Cohen-Macaulay Type via Lattice Homology and the Motivic Poincaré Series Thanks: The authors are partially supported by “Élvonal (Frontier)” Grant KKP 144148
Alex Hof ∗, András Némethi †

Abstract
. We give results on reduced complex-analytic curve germs which relate their indecomposable maximal Cohen-Macaulay (MCM) modules to their lattice homology groups and related invariants, thereby providing a connection between the algebraic theory of MCM modules and techniques arising from low-dimensional topology. In particular, we characterize the germs ( C , o ) (C,o) of finite Cohen-Macaulay type in terms of the lattice homology ℍ ∗ ​ ( C , o ) \mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o) , and those of tame type in terms of the lattice homologies and associated spectral sequences of ( C , o ) (C,o) and its subcurves, including the distinction between germs of finite and infinite growth. As a consequence of these results, we obtain corresponding characterizations of a germ’s Cohen-Macaulay type in terms of the motivic Poincaré series.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary. 13C14, 13D40, 32S05, 32S10; Secondary. 14B05, 32S25, 57K10, 57K14.

1.
Introduction

The
maximal Cohen-Macaulay (MCM) modules
over a given Noetherian local ring are those with depth equal to the largest possible value, its Krull dimension
d
—if one wishes, these can be characterized more geometrically as the modules with local cohomology concentrated in degree
d
. The project of understanding such rings, and the space germs to which they correspond, through classification of their MCM modules is a longstanding one, originating in the works of Auslander, Drozd, Reiten, and Roiter (
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
24
]
) from the perspective of representation theory and subsequently developed further by these mathematicians and others, including Artin, Buchweitz, Dieterich, Eisenbud, Green, Greuel, Herzog, Knörrer, Reiner, and Schreyer—for these references, and a general introduction to the topic, see
[
54
]
or
[
38
]
.

Our particular interest will be in these questions as they pertain to reduced complex-analytic curve germs and the corresponding convergent power series rings. In this setting, as in others (cf.
[
18
]
), the objects can be grouped into three main classes: those of
finite Cohen-Macaulay (CM) type
, which admit only finitely many indecomposable MCM modules up to isomorphism, those of
tame CM type
, which have infinitely many such modules but only finitely many 1-parameter families of them of any given rank, and those of
wild CM type
, which exhibit families of unbounded dimension in each rank (
[
20
]
). These properties reflect the geometry of such curve germs in surprising ways—for example, it turns out that those which are Gorenstein and of finite CM type are precisely the simple (i.e.,
ADE
) germs.

Among the main general tools for identifying the CM types of germs in this context are
dominance conditions
, which characterize germs of finite and tame CM type as those which birationally dominate certain foundational plane curve germs (respectively, the
ADE
and
T_{pq}
germs—see Propositions
4.2.3
and
4.4.1
), and
overring conditions
, which give algebraic criteria in terms of the interactions between various subrings of the integral closures of the local rings under consideration (see Propositions
4.2.4
and
4.4.2
). Though powerful, both types of conditions have limitations—the dominance conditions are conceptually straightforward but difficult to verify in practice, while the overring conditions are more computationally tractable but theoretically somewhat opaque, and both are formulated using analytic objects (subrings and overrings) which exhibit positive-dimensional moduli. Our purpose in this note will be to supplement them with easy-to-check conditions more readily relatable to other analytic and topological properties of the germ in question. These will be phrased in terms of discrete invariants of curve germs which have been the subject of recent work in a variety of other directions and thus serve as a bridge between the algebraic considerations of Cohen-Macaulay theory and the cohomology theories and other data arising from low-dimensional topology—specifically, these invariants are the
lattice homology groups
(together with the associated spectral sequences) and
motivic Poincaré series
.

Lattice (co)homology theories originated in the study of normal surface singularities (
[
46
]
), where they provide a singularity-theoretic means of computing 3-manifold invariants of a given singularity’s link, such as its Heegaard Floer homology (
[
55
]
) and Seiberg-Witten invariant (
[
47
]
); later work has produced similar definitions in a variety of related contexts, including isolated singularities of sufficiently large dimension (
[
2
]
) and singularities endowed with finite collections of generalized valuations (
[
50
]
). For our purposes, the relevant invariant will be the
analytic lattice homology of reduced curve singularities
, which was essentially introduced in
[
3
]
(see also
[
4
,
36
]
), albeit in cohomological terms; the homological formulation, as well as its
level filtration
and the corresponding spectral sequence, which we will also make use of, can be found in
[
49
]
.

The essential concept of this branch of the theory is that the components of the normalization of a given curve germ give rise to a lattice, which is endowed with a
weight filtration
determined by the interaction of the corresponding valuations with the normalization map; the various pieces of homological information we consider then arise as invariants of the corresponding filtered cubical complex. This provides a powerful general framework for encoding invariants of the curve itself in terms of discrete data—for example, the delta invariant appears as the Euler characteristic of the lattice homology. Likewise, the motivic Poincaré series can be obtained as the result of making certain substitutions in a power series determined by a refinement of the spectral sequence’s
E^{1}
-page, which was demonstrated in
[
49
]
; the core ideas behind the relationship originally appeared in
[
29
]
, where a relationship between the spectral sequence and the Heegaard Floer link homology of the link is established, albeit in different language, for algebraic plane curve germs.

Our first step in fitting the CM types of curve germs into this picture takes the form of the following theorem, which uses machinery introduced in Definitions
2.1.3
(the weight function
w_{0}^{C}
) and
5.3.3
(the notion of a minimal spectral cycle), as well as the classification discussed in Subsection
3.1
(note our inclusion of the nonsingular germ
A_{0}
in the
ADE
family):

Theorem 1
.

Let
(C,o)
be a reduced complex-analytic curve germ. Then
(C,o)
is of finite Cohen-Macaulay type if and only if
\min w_{0}^{C}\geq-1
.

More specifically, we have the following equivalences:

•

(C,o)
birationally dominates a simple germ of type
A_{n}
(for some
n\geq 0
) if and only if
\min w_{0}^{C}=0
. (In fact, in this case,
(C,o)
is an
A_{n}
germ.)

•

(C,o)
birationally dominates a simple singularity of type
D_{n}
(for some
n\geq 4
), but no simple germ of type
A_{n}
(for any
n\geq 0
), if and only if
\min w_{0}^{C}=-1
and
(C,o)
has a minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight 0.

•

(C,o)
birationally dominates a simple singularity of type
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
, or
E_{8}
, but no simple germ of type
A_{n}
(for any
n\geq 0
) or
D_{n}
(for any
n\geq 4
), if and only if
\min w_{0}^{C}=-1
and
(C,o)
does not have a minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight 0.

Proof.

This will follow by Theorem
7.1.2
and Propositions
8.1.1
and
8.2.1
.
∎

Thus, since the minimum value of the weight function is visible on the level of the lattice homology
\mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o)
by (
5.1.3
), computing these homology groups is enough to determine whether
(C,o)
is of finite CM type. (Alternately, we will see in Proposition
7.1.1
that one can also read this information directly from the weights of certain lattice points.) Moreover, the latter part of the theorem indicates that we can distinguish between the different dominance classes among the finite-CM-type germs by supplementing the homology with fairly coarse data about the shape of the
E^{1}
-page of the associated spectral sequence; this will be needed for our subsequent discussion regarding the tame classification.

To wit, we obtain a characterization of tame-CM-type germs, and of the subclass of those which are of
finite growth
, in these terms—note for the latter that we use the notion of “maximal rank” given in Definition
5.3.7
:

Theorem 2
.

Let
(C,o)
be a reduced complex-analytic curve germ, with
(C,o)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}(C_{i},o)
the decomposition into irreducible components. Then
(C,o)
is of tame Cohen-Macaulay type if and only if all of the following conditions hold:

(a)

\min w_{0}^{C}=-2
.

(b)

(C,o)
has a minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight
-1
.

(c)

For each
1\leq i\leq r
,
(C_{i},o)
birationally dominates (and thus is) a simple germ of type
A_{n}
(for some
n\geq 0
).

(d)

For each
1\leq i\leq r
,
(\hat{C}_{i},o):=\bigcup_{j\neq i}(C_{j},o)
birationally dominates a simple germ of type
A_{n}
(for some
n\geq 0
) or
D_{n}
(for some
n\geq 4
).

If these conditions hold,
(C,o)
is of finite growth if and only if the group
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)
of minimal spectral 1-cycles of weight
-1
is of maximal rank.

Proof.

See Subsection
10.3
.
∎

Note that, by Theorem
1
, Conditions (c) and (d) can be understood in terms of the lattice homology groups and spectral sequences of the subcurves
C_{i}
and
\hat{C}_{i}
respectively for
1\leq i\leq r
; as such, tameness is determined by the homology groups
\mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o)
,
\mathbb{H}_{*}(C_{i},o)
, and
\mathbb{H}_{*}(\hat{C}_{i},o)
together with the knowledge of which of the groups
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)
and
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(\hat{C}_{i},o)
(when defined) are nonzero. (As in the finite case, we can also identify a tame germ by computing the weights of a few well-chosen lattice points—see Propositions
7.1.1
and
9.2.6
.)

Remark 1.0.1
.

Some of the conditions of the preceding theorem can be refined slightly or restated. In particular, as long as Condition (a) holds, we have:

•

Condition (b) is automatically satisfied if
(C,o)
has multiplicity 4 and more than 2 branches. (See Proposition
10.1.2
.)

•

Condition (d) is automatically satisfied unless
(C,o)
has multiplicity 4. Moreover, in this case, the condition still holds immediately for all indices
1\leq i\leq r
such that
(C_{i},o)
is not smooth. (See Lemma
9.2.4
.)

•

By similar reasoning, we can see that any subcurve obtained by removing two or more branches from
(C,o)
dominates an
A_{n}
germ (for some
n\geq 0
), so Conditions (c) and (d) can be replaced with:

(cd)

Any reduced proper subcurve of
(C,o)
birationally dominates a simple germ of type
A_{n}
(for some
n\geq 0
) or
D_{n}
(for some
n\geq 4
).

Theorems
1
and
2
together give us a characterization of the CM types of curve germs in terms of lattice-homological information about them and their subcurves. As we will see in Section
11
, such information can also be reformulated purely in terms of the corresponding
motivic Poincaré series
, without explicit reference to the theory of lattice homology. Consequently, we find that these invariants can also be used to detect CM type as well; we give the explicit conditions in Theorem
3
(see Subsection
11.3
).

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. Sections
2
through
5
give the preliminary definitions and results on which our main arguments will be based. Section
2
establishes our basic notations and develops the machinery of the weighted lattice upon which lattice homology of a given curve germ is based, while Section
3
recounts some background information about the birational dominance relation and classifications of plane curve germs up to various forms of equivalence. Section
4
details the relevant notions from the theory of Cohen-Macaulay modules, and Section
5
contains the main definitions and results pertaining to lattice homology, including the level filtration and associated spectral sequence—in particular, Subsection
5.3
develops the concept of minimal spectral cycles, which is original to the present work.

The remaining sections are largely concerned with building up the results necessary to prove our main theorems. Section
6
gives computations of the weighted lattices associated to plane curve germs in the foundational
ADE
and
T_{pq}
families. Section
7
establishes the characterization of finite-CM-type germs in terms of lattice homology, while Section
8
details the means of distinguishing between these based on which
ADE
germs they dominate. Section
9
gives a characterization of the tameness of germs in terms of their weighted lattices, which is then translated into the homological framework in Section
10
; Section
11
gives the reformulation of these results in terms of the motivic Poincaré series. Finally, Section
12
gives an application of our techniques to the classification of plane curve germs—in particular, our results in this section allow us to distinguish between the various subclasses (i.e.,
A
,
D
,
E
, parabolic, hyperbolic, and unimodal exceptional) of the simple and unimodal germs in terms of lattice-homological invariants.

2.
Preliminaries. Invariants of reduced curve germs

In this section, we introduce some notation and definitions which will be used throughout this paper and recall some preliminary properties of reduced complex-analytic curve germs.

2.1.
Normalization, valuations, and the Hilbert function (
[
3
,
4
,
36
]
)

Definition 2.1.1
.

Let
(C,o)
be a reduced complex-analytic curve germ,
(\mathscr{O}=\mathscr{O}_{C,o},\mathfrak{m})
its local ring at
o
, and
\{(C_{i},o)\}_{i=1}^{r}
its irreducible components. Also let
\nu:({\overline{C}},{\overline{o}})\cong\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{r}({\overline{C_{i}}},{\overline{o}}_{i})\to(C,o)
be the normalization, where
({\overline{C}},{\overline{o}})
is a multigerm with
r
smooth components
({\overline{C_{i}}},{\overline{o}}_{i})
. The corresponding ring
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{C}\{t_{i}\}
is the integral closure of
\mathscr{O}
in its total fraction ring. Then each component
(C_{i},o)
provides a valuation on
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
and hence on the subring
\mathscr{O}
as well; explicitly, for each
1\leq i\leq r
and
f\in{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
, we denote by
\mathfrak{v}_{i}(f)
the order of vanishing
{\rm ord}_{t_{i}}(f)
of the restriction of
f
to
({\overline{C_{i}}},{\overline{o}}_{i})
at
{\overline{o}}_{i}
.

We define the
lattice of
(C,o)
to be
\mathbb{N}^{r}
, so that the coordinates correspond to the branches of
(C,o)
; for each
1\leq i\leq r
, we let
e^{i}:=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
denote the
i^{\text{th}}
basis vector, and set
e:=\sum_{i=1}^{r}e^{i}=(1,\ldots,1)
. We also let
m_{i}
be the multiplicity of
(C_{i},o)
at
o
for each
i
and define the
multiplicity vector

m:=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{r})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}e^{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
; where necessary, we may denote this by
m(C,o)
. The lattice indexes descending
lattice filtrations
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathscr{F}
of
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
and
\mathscr{O}
respectively by ideals, given by setting, for
\ell=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r})\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
,

{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell):=\{f\in{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\mid\mathfrak{v}_{i}(f)\geq\ell_{i}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq r\}=({t_{1}}^{\ell_{1}},\ldots,{t_{r}}^{\ell_{r}}){\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\text{ and}

\mathscr{F}(\ell):={\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)\cap\mathscr{O}=\{f\in\mathscr{O}\mid\mathfrak{v}_{i}(f)\geq\ell_{i}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq r\}.

Proposition 2.1.2
.

The following properties of the lattice filtrations are immediate:

•

For each
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
such that
\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}
(that is,
\ell_{i}\leq\ell_{i}^{\prime}
for all
1\leq i\leq r
),
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)\supseteq{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell^{\prime})
and hence
\mathscr{F}(\ell)\supseteq\mathscr{F}(\ell^{\prime})
too. Moreover,
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\min\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\})={\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell^{\prime})
and
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell+\ell^{\prime})={\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell){\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell^{\prime})

for any
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
.

•

For each
\ell=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r})\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
,
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{C}\{t_{i}\}/({t_{i}}^{\ell_{i}})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\ell_{i}=|\ell|
.

•

{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(km)=\mathfrak{m}^{k}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
for any
k\geq 0
and
\mathscr{F}(m)=(\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}})\cap\mathscr{O}=\mathfrak{m}
.

Definition 2.1.3
.

The
Hilbert functions

\mathfrak{h},{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}:\mathbb{N}^{r}\to\mathbb{N}
associated with the filtrations and the
weight function

w_{0}:\mathbb{N}^{r}\to\mathbb{Z}
(the main ingredient of the lattice homology) are defined as follows for each lattice point
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
:

•

\mathfrak{h}(\ell):=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\mathscr{O}}{\mathscr{F}(\ell)}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathscr{O}}{{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)}
.

•

{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\ell):=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}}{{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathscr{O}}=|\ell|-\mathfrak{h}(\ell)
.

•

w_{0}(\ell):=\mathfrak{h}(\ell)-{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\ell)=2\mathfrak{h}(\ell)-|\ell|=|\ell|-2{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\ell)
.

We may also indicate
(C,o)
in the notation by writing
\mathfrak{h}^{C},{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}\!\!\!\phantom{\mathfrak{h}}^{C},w^{C}_{0}
in place of
\mathfrak{h},{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}},w_{0}
.

A straightforward computation shows that, for arbitrary
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
, we have

(2.1.4)

w_{0}(\ell+\ell^{\prime})-w_{0}(\ell)=|\ell^{\prime}|-2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\ (\,{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathscr{O}\,)/(\,{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell+\ell^{\prime})+\mathscr{O}\,).

2.2.
Connection with the semigroup of values (
[
16
,
17
,
27
]
)

Definition 2.2.1
.

The
semigroup of values
of
(C,o)
is the subsemigroup of
(\mathbb{N}^{r},+)
given by

\mathscr{S}_{C}:=\big\{\,\big(\mathfrak{v}_{1}(g),\dots,\mathfrak{v}_{r}(g)\big)\in\mathbb{N}^{r}\ \big|\ g\in\mathscr{O}\text{ is not a zero-divisor}\,\big\}.

This semigroup, which we may also denote simply by
\mathscr{S}
, is known to possess many nice properties—for example, if
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathscr{S}
, then
\min\{s_{1},s_{2}\}\in\mathscr{S}
as well. See, e.g.,
[
16
, p. 350]
or
[
36
, 2.8]
for details. The Hilbert function
\mathfrak{h}
determines the semigroup
\mathscr{S}
as follows:

(2.2.2)

\mathscr{S}=\{\,\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}\mid\mathfrak{h}(\ell+e^{i})>\mathfrak{h}(\ell)\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq r\,\}.

Conversely, the semigroup determines the Hilbert function as well. For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
, set

\overline{\Delta}_{i}(\ell):=\{\,s\in\mathscr{S}\mid s_{i}=\ell_{i}\text{ and }s_{j}\geq\ell_{j}\text{ for all }j\not=i\,\}.

Then, for all
1\leq i\leq r
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
, one has

(2.2.3)

\mathfrak{h}(0)=0\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \mathfrak{h}(\ell+e^{i})-\mathfrak{h}(\ell)=\begin{cases}\ 1\ \text{ if }\ \overline{\Delta}_{i}(\ell)\not=\emptyset,\\
\ 0\ \text{ if }\ \overline{\Delta}_{i}(\ell)=\emptyset.\end{cases}

In particular, if
(C,o)
is irreducible, then
\mathfrak{h}(\ell)=\#\{s<\ell\mid s\in\mathscr{S}\}
for each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}=\mathbb{N}
.

Since the Hilbert function determines the weight, we obtain a similar characterization of
w_{0}
in terms of
\mathscr{S}
:

(2.2.4)

w_{0}(0)=0\ \ \text{ and }\ \ w_{0}(\ell+e^{i})-w_{0}(\ell)=\begin{cases}\ +1\ \text{ if }\ \overline{\Delta}_{i}(\ell)\not=\emptyset,\\
\ -1\ \text{ if }\ \overline{\Delta}_{i}(\ell)=\emptyset.\end{cases}

In particular,
-|\ell|\leq w_{0}(\ell)\leq|\ell|
and
w_{0}(\ell)
is equivalent modulo 2 to
|\ell|
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
.

For future use, we note the following reinterpretation of (
2.2.3
) and (
2.2.4
):

Lemma 2.2.5
.

For each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
and
1\leq i\leq r
,
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell+e^{i}))
is a principal ideal of
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell+e^{i})
. If
w_{0}(\ell+e^{i})=w_{0}(\ell)+1
and
g\in{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
is such that
g({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell+e^{i}))={\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell+e^{i}))
, then there exists
f\in\mathscr{O}
such that
f
is equivalent to
g
modulo
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell+e^{i})
.

We now note some consequences of our characterizations of
\mathfrak{h}
and
w_{0}
by the semigroup. Since
\mathscr{S}\setminus\{0\}\subseteq m+\mathbb{N}^{r}
, (
2.2.4
) implies that, for all of the lattice points
0<\ell\leq m
,

(2.2.6)

w_{0}(\ell)=2-|\ell|.

Applying this identity for
\ell=m
and using (
2.1.4
), we find that

(2.2.7)

w_{0}(2m)=2-2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\ (\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O})/(\mathfrak{m}^{2}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}).

Finally, since the Hilbert function is defined by a valuative filtration,
\mathfrak{h}
and
w_{0}
satisfy the
matroid rank inequality
for lattice points
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
:

(2.2.8)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{h}(\ell)+\mathfrak{h}(\ell^{\prime})&\geq\mathfrak{h}\big(\min\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}\big)+\mathfrak{h}\big(\max\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}\big),\\
w_{0}(\ell)+w_{0}(\ell^{\prime})&\geq w_{0}\big(\min\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}\big)+w_{0}\big(\max\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}\big).\end{split}

2.3.
The conductor and the
\delta
-invariant

Definition 2.3.1
.

The
delta invariant
of
(C,o)
is
\delta=\delta(C,o):=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\mathscr{O}}/\mathscr{O}
. Let
\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}(C,o):=(\mathscr{O}:{\overline{\mathscr{O}}})
be the
conductor ideal
of the normalization—i.e., the largest ideal of
\mathscr{O}
which is also an ideal of
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
. This ideal in
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
has the form
({t_{1}}^{c_{1}},\ldots,{t_{r}}^{c_{r}}){\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
; the corresponding lattice point
c=c(C,o):=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{r})\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
is called the
conductor
of the semigroup
\mathscr{S}
.

This
c
is the smallest lattice point (with respect to the usual coordinate-wise partial order on
\mathbb{N}^{r}
) with the property
c+\mathbb{N}^{r}\subseteq\mathscr{S}
. In particular,
\mathscr{F}(c)={\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(c)=\mathfrak{c}
and so
\mathfrak{h}(c)=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{O}/\mathfrak{c}
; this is also equal to
|c|-\delta
since
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathfrak{c}=|c|
. Note that
(C,o)
is smooth if and only if any of the equivalent conditions
\mathfrak{c}=\mathscr{O}
,
\mathfrak{c}={\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
, and
\mathscr{O}={\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
holds.

By
[
36
, Theorem 4.1.1]
, the Gorenstein property is reflected in the symmetry of the weight function on the part of
\mathbb{N}^{r}
below the conductor:

(2.3.2)

(C,o)\text{ is Gorenstein }\Leftrightarrow\ w_{0}(\ell)=w_{0}(c-\ell)\text{ for any }0\leq\ell\leq c.

2.4.
Subcurves and their invariants (
[
29
, Section 3.4]
)

We can define, for any
J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}
, the subcurve
(C_{J},o):=\bigcup_{i\in J}(C_{i},o)
. There is a natural inclusion
\iota_{J}
of the lattice
\mathbb{N}^{|J|}
of
(C_{J},o)
into the lattice
\mathbb{N}^{r}
of
(C,o)
(where, for
i\not\in J
, the
i
-entries of the elements of
\mathbb{N}^{|J|}
regarded in
\mathbb{N}^{r}
are taken to be zero). Then
\mathfrak{h}^{C}(\iota_{J}(\ell))=\mathfrak{h}^{C_{J}}(\ell)
, and hence
w_{0}^{C}(\iota_{J}(\ell))=w_{0}^{C_{J}}(\ell)
, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{|J|}
.

3.
Preliminaries. Relationships between reduced curve germs

Here we recall some facts about the ways in which reduced complex-analytic curve germs relate to one another.

3.1.
(Classical) classification of reduced plane curve germs

When we study complex-analytic curve germs abstractly, we of course treat them up to isomorphism in the usual sense—or, equivalently, up to isomorphism of the corresponding local
\mathbb{C}
-algebras. On the other hand, if we are interested specifically in those which are also
hypersurface
germs—that is, in plane curve germs—then we also have the information of the embedding into
(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
or, more stringently, of a defining function germ
f:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C},0)
, and we can consider this up to various notions of equivalence. One notion is
contact
, or
\mathscr{K}\!\!
-equivalence
, which turns out to coincide with isomorphism as abstract (non-embedded) germs (see, e.g.,
[
40
]
); more narrowly, we can also use
right
or
\mathscr{R}
-equivalence
, which takes
(C,o)=(\{f=0\},0)
to be equivalent to another plane curve germ
(\{g=0\},0)
if and only if there is a local analytic diffeomorphism
\phi:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
such that
f=g\circ\phi
(see, e.g.,
[
5
,
40
,
52
]
).

Arnol’d and his school classified up to
\mathscr{R}
-equivalence all simple, unimodal, and bimodal hypersurface germs (
[
5
]
); although they worked more broadly, we restrict our discussions about hypersurface germs to reduced plane curve germs (that is, germs with corank
\leq 2
). The non-smooth simple ones are the
ADE
germs, namely
A_{n}
(
n\geq 1
) with normal form
f(x,y)=x^{n+1}+y^{2}
;
D_{n}
(
n\geq 4
):
f(x,y)=x^{2}y+y^{n-1}
;
E_{6}
:
f(x,y)=x^{3}+y^{4}
;
E_{7}
:
f(x,y)=x^{3}+xy^{3}
;
E_{8}
:
f(x,y)=x^{3}+y^{5}
(though sometimes we will use other local equations, more convenient
in certain situations). For convenience, we will also denote the smooth germ by
A_{0}
and regard it as part of the
A_{n}
family (with local equation
f(x,y)=x+y^{2}
corresponding to
n=0
).

The
\mathscr{R}
-unimodal germs are divided into three groups: parabolic, hyperbolic, and exceptional. The parabolic and hyperbolic germs can be denoted uniformly: they are called
T_{p,q,2}
singularities, with normal form
f(x,y)=x^{p}+y^{q}+\lambda x^{2}y^{2}
, where
p\leq q
are integers such that
1/p+1/q\leq 1/2
and
\lambda
is the modality parameter. There are two cases with
1/p+1/q=1/2
, namely
(p,q)=(4,4)
and
(3,6)
; for the
\mathscr{R}
-modality parameter, one has to impose
\lambda^{2}\not=4
in the first case and
4\lambda^{3}+27\not=0
in the second case. These are the parabolic germs. All other
T_{p,q,2}
germs are hyperbolic, with
1/p+1/q<1/2
, and for them we have
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
(
[
5
]
).

\mathscr{R}
-equivalence of germs implies
\mathscr{K}\!\!
-equivalence; however, the two classifications do not agree. The family of
\mathscr{K}\!\!
-simple germs coincides with the
\mathscr{R}
-simple germs—however, the set of
\mathscr{K}\!\!
-unimodal germs is larger than the
\mathscr{R}
-unimodal set, and here it can also happen that different
\mathscr{R}
-classes collapse into the same
\mathscr{K}\!\!
-class. For example, for a given
p
and
q
, all the local algebras of
T_{p,q,2}
in the hyperbolic case are isomorphic independently of
\lambda
, so we can take
\lambda=1
as a representative of the
\mathscr{K}\!\!
-equivalence class. In the parabolic cases, the unimodality parameter
\lambda
survives (
[
52
]
).

Following
[
19
,
21
]
, we will use the uniform notation
T_{pq}
for the
\mathscr{K}\!\!
-equivalence classes
T_{p,q,2}
; if necessary, we write
T_{pq}(\lambda)
to indicate the unimodality parameter.

3.2.
Birational maps and dominance

The central role played by the normalization map is the most important example of the general observation that we can understand the structure of and relationships among reduced curve germs and multigerms in terms of birational maps between them. In particular, the following relation will be crucial in the sequel:

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let
(C,o)
and
(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})
be reduced complex-analytic curve singularities with local algebras
\mathscr{O}
and
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}
respectively, and let
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
be the ring corresponding to the normalization of
\mathscr{O}
. We say that
(C^{\prime},o)
birationally dominates
(C,o)
if and only if there is a finite birational map
(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})\to(C,o)
of complex-analytic space germs, or, equivalently, there exists a factorization
\mathscr{O}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
of the natural embedding
\mathscr{O}\hookrightarrow{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
.

The following list brings together some immediate features:

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Suppose that
(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})
birationally dominates
(C,o)
.

(a)

Any germ
(C^{\prime\prime},o^{\prime\prime})
dominating
(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})
also dominates
(C,o)
; that is, birational dominance is transitive.

(b)

The number of local irreducible components of
(C,o)
and
(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})
agree.

(c)

The normalizations of
(C,o)
and
(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})

can be identified, and hence the corresponding lattices can be identified too.

(d)

The multiplicity vectors (in the identified lattice
\mathbb{N}^{r}
) satisfy
m(C,o)\geq m(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})
.

(e)

\mathscr{S}_{C,o}\subseteq\mathscr{S}_{C^{\prime},o^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{r}
.

(f)

\delta(C,o)\geq\delta(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})
.

(g)

\mathfrak{h}^{C}(\ell)\leq\mathfrak{h}^{C^{\prime}}(\ell)
, and hence
w^{C}_{0}(\ell)\leq w_{0}^{C^{\prime}}(\ell)
too, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
.

In general, for a given germ
(C,o)
, the classification of all the germs

(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})
(and the corresponding birational maps) which birationally dominate
(C,o)
is not very easy.

Examples 3.2.3
.

We give some observations:

(1)

Although part (e) of the preceding result is very useful for ruling out birational dominance, its converse does not hold; that is, it cannot be used to show birational dominance. Indeed, birational dominance is not a combinatorial property—for instance, if
T_{4,4}(\lambda)
birationally dominates
T_{4,4}(\lambda^{\prime})
, we can see that the dominance map must be an isomorphism as follows. A
T_{4,4}
singularity is the union of four distinct smooth branches through the origin in the plane, and so the normality of smooth germs implies that the restriction of our birational map to any component defines an isomorphism to the corresponding component of the codomain. Since the tangent spaces to these branches at the origin span those of the ambient planes, our map thus gives an isomorphism when extended to a map of these planes, and so it itself is also an isomorphism. In particular, by
[
52
]
, this dominance implies that
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}
, even though all
T_{4,4}
singularities have the same semigroup.

(2)

The fact that
(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})
dominates
(C,o)
does not necessarily imply that their embedding dimensions satisfy
\edim(C,o)\geq\edim(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})
. For example, any plane curve singularity with three irreducible components is dominated by the space curve
\{xy=yz=zx=0\}
, since the normalization factors through it.

(3)

A_{n}
dominates
A_{n+2}
by the ‘blow up’ map
(x,y)\mapsto(x,xy)
for all
n\geq 0
,
A_{0}
dominates any irreducible curve germ by the normalization map, and
A_{1}=\{xy=0\}
dominates any
(C,o)
with
r=2
—e.g., it dominates any
D_{2k+1}=\{y(x^{2}-y^{2k-1})=0\}
by
(x,y)\mapsto(y^{2k-1},x+y^{2})
.
D_{n}
dominates
D_{n+2}
by
(x,y)\mapsto(xy,y)
for all
n\geq 4
.

By Lemma
3.2.2
(d)
,
D_{m}
cannot dominate
A_{n}
for
m
and
n
odd, even though
r=2
in both cases. However, it is also true that
A_{n}
cannot dominate
D_{m}
for any
m
whenever
n\geq 2
by Lemma
3.2.2
(e)
:
\mathscr{S}(D_{m})\ni(2,1)\not\in\mathscr{S}(A_{n})
(up to a reordering of branches). Similarly,
A_{n}
for
n\geq 2
or
D_{5}
cannot dominate
E_{7}
.

(4)

There is a complete classification in
[
31
]
(see also
[
26
]
) of those curve singularities which birationally dominate an
ADE
germ. Each is either of type
ADE
or non-Gorenstein with local algebra
\mathscr{O}
of one of the following forms:
\mathbb{C}\{x,y,z\}/(x^{2}-y^{n},xz,yz)
for
n\geq 2
;

\mathbb{C}\{t^{3},t^{4},t^{5}\}\subset\mathbb{C}\{t\}
;

\mathbb{C}\{x,y,z\}/(x^{3}-y^{4},xz-y^{2},y^{2}z-x^{2},yz^{2}-xy)
;

\mathbb{C}\{t^{3},t^{5},t^{7}\}\subset\mathbb{C}\{t\}
.

4.
Preliminaries. Cohen-Macaulay properties

Here we recall the basic definitions of the theory of maximal Cohen-Macaulay modules, as well as some foundational results which will be important for our purposes. For the sake of simplicity, we will concern ourselves mainly with the case of the local ring of a complex-analytic curve singularity, and avoid stating definitions and results in the utmost generality.

4.1.
Cohen-Macaulay modules and local rings

Definition 4.1.1

(
[
12
,
54
]
)
.

Let
(\mathscr{O},\mathfrak{m})
be a Noetherian local ring and
M
a finitely-generated nonzero
\mathscr{O}
-module. A sequence
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\in\mathfrak{m}
is said to be
M
-regular
if
x_{i}
is a non-zero divisor on
M/(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1})M
for all
1\leq i\leq k
; the length
k
of the longest such sequence is called the
depth of
M
.

M
is called a
Cohen-Macaulay module
, or
CM module
, if its depth is equal to the dimension of its support; if, moreover,
\dim\supp M=\dim\mathscr{O}
,
M
is said to be a
maximal Cohen-Macaulay module
, or simply
MCM module
.
\mathscr{O}
is called a
Cohen-Macaulay local ring
if it is a CM module (and hence MCM module) over itself.

It is known (see
[
54
, Proposition 1.5]
, noting the difference in terminology for MCM modules) that if
\mathscr{O}
is a reduced local ring of dimension one—as with the rings
\mathscr{O}_{C,o}
we consider—then an
\mathscr{O}
-module
M
is MCM if and only if it is
torsion-free
in the sense that the natural map
M\to M^{\vee\vee}
to its double dual is injective.

Recall that a module is called
indecomposable
when
it has no nontrivial direct summands. We have the following structure theorem for local analytic algebras and their MCM modules:

Proposition 4.1.2

(e.g.,
[
54
, 1.3 & 1.18]
)
.

The direct sum of MCM modules is MCM, and conversely any direct summand of an MCM module is MCM. If
(C,o)
is a reduced complex-analytic curve germ, any finitely-generated module over
\mathscr{O}_{C,o}
admits an essentially unique decomposition into finitely many indecomposable direct summands.

4.2.
Local rings of finite CM type (
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
31
,
34
,
38
,
54
]
)

Definition 4.2.1
.

Let
(\mathscr{O},\mathfrak{m})
be a Noetherian local ring. We say that
\mathscr{O}
is of
finite Cohen-Macaulay type
(or
CM type
) if there are, up to isomorphism, only finitely many indecomposable MCM modules on
\mathscr{O}
. Otherwise, we say that
\mathscr{O}
is of
infinite Cohen-Macaulay type
.

If
(C,o)
is a germ of a complex-analytic space, we define the
Cohen-Macaulay type of
(C,o)
to be that of the corresponding local ring
\mathscr{O}_{C,o}
.

Remark 4.2.2
.

Note that, here and throughout the rest of this note, we use the phrase “Cohen-Macaulay type” in the sense of, e.g.,
[
21
]
—some authors instead reserve this term for the type of a ring as a module over itself in the sense of
[
12
, Definition 1.2.15]
, but we do not adopt this convention.

In the reduced curve case which is our primary focus, the following two propositions provide characterizations of the germs of finite CM type in terms of birational dominance and of the algebraic properties of the normalization map respectively (see, e.g.,
[
54
, Ch. 9]
):

Proposition 4.2.3

(
[
31
]
)
.

Let
(C,o)
be a reduced complex-analytic curve germ. Then
(C,o)
is of finite Cohen-Macaulay type if and only if it birationally dominates a simple (i.e.,
ADE
) curve germ.

(Recall that in Example
3.2.3
(3) we listed every curve singularity which dominates at least one
ADE
germ. For example, the curve with local ring
\mathbb{C}\{t^{3},t^{5},t^{7}\}
dominates
E_{8}
, which has local ring
\mathbb{C}\{t^{3},t^{5}\}
.)

Proposition 4.2.4

(
[
24
,
30
,
33
]
)
.

Let
(C,o)
be a reduced complex-analytic curve germ with local algebra
(\mathscr{O},\mathfrak{m})
, and let
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
be the integral closure of
\mathscr{O}
in its total fraction ring. Then
(C,o)
is of finite Cohen-Macaulay type if and only if both of the following conditions hold:

(a)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\leq 3
(or, per Section
2
,
|m|\leq 3
).

(b)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\,(\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O})/(\mathfrak{m}^{2}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O})\leq 1
(cf. (
2.2.7
)).

4.3.
Local rings of infinite CM type

We can further distinguish between rings of infinite CM type using the behavior of
families
of indecomposable MCM modules (in the sense of
[
20
, Section 3]
):

Definition 4.3.1

(
[
19
,
20
,
21
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be the local ring of a reduced complex-analytic curve germ
(C,o)
. Then
\mathscr{O}
and, correspondingly,
(C,o)
are said to be of

•

tame Cohen-Macaulay type
when the indecomposable MCM
\mathscr{O}
-modules of any fixed rank, considered up to isomorphism, form a finite number of 1-parameter families, possibly together with a finite set of isolated modules.

•

wild Cohen-Macaulay type
when the indecomposable MCM
\mathscr{O}
-modules of any fixed rank include
n
-parameter families of non-isomorphic modules for arbitrarily large
n
.

The key classification result for curve germs of infinite CM type is the following (although see Remark
4.4.4
for a technical clarification):

Theorem 4.3.2

(
Tame-wild dichotomy

[
20
,
19
]
)
.

A reduced complex-analytic curve germ of infinite CM type is either of tame CM type or of wild CM type, but not both.

In the case of the germs which are of tame CM type, we can introduce one further distinction based on the numbers of the 1-parameter families under consideration:

Definition 4.3.3

(
[
21
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be the local ring of a reduced complex-analytic curve germ
(C,o)
of tame CM type. For each rank
r\in\mathbb{N}
, let
n_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
be the number of 1-parameter families of indecomposable MCM
\mathscr{O}
-modules, as considered in Definition
4.3.1
. Then we say that
\mathscr{O}
and
(C,o)
are of
finite growth
if there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
n_{r}\leq N
for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
; otherwise, we say they are of
infinite growth
.

4.4.
Conditions for tameness

We have the following analogue for Proposition
4.2.3
:

Proposition 4.4.1

(
[
21
]
)
.

Let
(C,o)
be a reduced complex-analytic curve germ of infinite CM type. Then
(C,o)
is of tame CM type if and only if it birationally dominates at least one
T_{pq}
singularity. Moreover,
(C,o)
is tame of finite growth if and only if it birationally dominates at least one of the parabolic singularities
T_{4,4}
and
T_{3,6}
.

Thus, the verification of the fact that a germ
(C,o)
is tame can be done in two steps; first one has to test that it
is indeed of infinite type—that is, it does not dominate an
ADE
germ (or, equivalently, at least one of the conditions from Proposition
4.2.4
fails)—then to prove that it dominates a
T_{pq}
germ. For any concrete germ, this second step can also be carried out algebraically using the following analogue of Proposition
4.2.4
:

Proposition 4.4.2

(
[
21
]
)
.

Let
(C,o)
be a reduced complex-analytic curve germ of infinite CM type, with
(\mathscr{O},\mathfrak{m})
,
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
, and
r
as in Section
2
.
Set
t:=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})
in
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cong\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{C}\{t_{i}\}
, and,
for each
1\leq i\leq r
, denote the corresponding idempotent
(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
by
\epsilon_{i}
. Also let
\theta\in\mathfrak{m}
be such that
\theta{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}=\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
. We define intermediate rings
\mathscr{O}\subseteq\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
(for all
1\leq i\leq r
) by
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}:=t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}
,
\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime}:=\theta t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}
, and
\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}:=\mathscr{O}^{\prime}+\mathbb{C}\epsilon_{i}
. For each ring
\mathscr{O}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}\subseteq{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
, we note by finiteness that we can write
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}/\mathfrak{m}\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}
as a product of finitely many Artinian local algebras; let
\lambda(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}})
be the vector of their lengths, under the convention that the entries are non-decreasing. (Thus
\lambda({\overline{\mathscr{O}}})
is, up to a reordering, the multiplicity vector
m=m(C,o)
.)

Then
(C,o)
is of tame CM type if and only if all of the following conditions hold:

(O1a)

|\lambda({\overline{\mathscr{O}}})|=|m|=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\leq 4
.

(O1b)

\lambda({\overline{\mathscr{O}}})\not\in\{(4),(1,3),(3)\}
.

(O2a)

|\lambda(\mathscr{O}^{\prime})|=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{O}^{\prime}/\mathfrak{m}\mathscr{O}^{\prime}\leq 3
.

(O2b)

For all
1\leq i\leq r
,
\lambda(\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime})\neq(1,3)
.

(O3)

If
|\lambda({\overline{\mathscr{O}}})|=|m|=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}=3
, then
|\lambda(\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime})|=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime}/\mathfrak{m}\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime}\leq 2
.

Note that here
\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}
is distinct from
(\mathscr{O}_{C_{i},o})^{\prime}
(unless
r=1
). Also observe that, although the isomorphism class of
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}

as a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
depends only on
r
,
\lambda(\mathscr{O}^{\prime})
is defined with respect to the
\mathscr{O}
-algebra structure induced by the embedding
\mathscr{O}\subseteq\mathscr{O}^{\prime}
, and so Condition (O2a) depends meaningfully on
(C,o)
.

Remark 4.4.3
.

In
[
21
]
, Condition (O2b) of the preceding result is stated in a slightly different way; the indices
i
are restricted to those satisfying the admissibility condition
\mathfrak{m}\epsilon_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{m}+\theta t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
. However, as noted in
[
21
, Remark 6.1]
, it is equivalent to require that the stated non-equality hold for all indices, and for our purposes this formulation will be more convenient. Ultimately, as discussed in Remark
1.0.1
, we will obtain an analogous but distinct optional restriction on the indices we consider in Condition (d) of Theorem
2
.

Remark 4.4.4
.

Strictly speaking, the results of
[
20
]
and
[
21
]
which we have discussed here—namely, Theorem
4.3.2
, Proposition
4.4.1
, and Proposition
4.4.2
—were originally stated and proved for
formal
power series rings, rather than the convergent power series rings we consider. However, it is possible to relax the completeness hypotheses used in these sources and apply the same arguments to rings which are instead merely Henselian, so these results still hold in our setting (
[
23
]
).

5.
Preliminaries. Lattice homology of reduced curve germs

In this section, we review the basic definitions pertaining to the
analytic lattice homology of curve germs
—for more details, see
[
3
,
4
,
35
,
36
,
49
,
37
]
. Also see
[
2
,
46
,
48
]
for other lattice (co)homology theories in singularity theory and, e.g.,
[
29
,
49
,
55
]
for Floer-theoretic interpretations in low-dimensional topology. Our discussion concludes with Subsection
5.3
, which introduces new machinery for working with certain features of the spectral sequence introduced in
[
49
]
.

5.1.
Definition of the lattice homology of reduced curve germs (
[
3
,
4
,
36
]
)

We fix a reduced complex-analytic curve germ
(C,o)
and adopt the conventions of Section
2
. In particular, the reader is invited to recall the weight function
w_{0}:\mathbb{N}^{r}\to\mathbb{Z}
introduced in Definition
2.1.3
; we now develop a homology theory based on the corresponding filtration of an associated cubical complex.

To wit, the positive orthant
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{r}
has a natural decomposition into cubes such that the set of zero-dimensional cubes consists of the lattice points
\mathbb{N}^{r}
; any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
and subset
I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}
of cardinality
q
then define a closed
q
-dimensional cube
(\ell,I)
of the decomposition, the convex hull in
\mathbb{R}^{r}
of the vertices
\ell+\sum_{i\in J}e^{i}
as
J
runs over all subsets of
I
. We may also refer to such a closed cube
(\ell,I)
as
\square=\square_{\ell,I}
, and we call
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{r}
together with this decomposition the
cubical complex of
(C,o)
.

Definition 5.1.1
.

We define the
weight
of a
q
-cube
\square
for any
q\geq 0
by

w_{q}(\square)=\max\{\,w_{0}(\ell)\mid\ell\in\square\cap\mathbb{N}^{r}\,\}

and the
weight-at-most-n space

S_{n}
of the weight function by

S_{n}=\bigcup\big\{\,\square\text{ a }q\text{-cube of }\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{r}\mid q\in\mathbb{N},w_{q}(\square)\leq n\,\big\}.

That is,
S_{n}
is the full subcomplex of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{r}
spanned by the vertices of weight at most
n
.

Since the set
\{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}\mid w_{0}(\ell)\leq n\}
is finite for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
(e.g., by (
2.2.4
) and the existence of the conductor
c
), each
S_{n}
is a finite closed cubical complex and
S_{n}=\emptyset
for
n\ll 0
. Moreover,
S_{n}\subseteq S_{n+1}
for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and indeed the
S_{n}
together comprise an increasing
\mathbb{Z}
-indexed filtration of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{r}
.

Definition 5.1.2
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
th
lattice homology
of the germ
(C,o)
is defined as

\mathbb{H}_{k}=\mathbb{H}_{k}(C,o):=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{k}(S_{n},\mathbb{Z});

we endow this with the structure of a
2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathbb{Z}[U]
-module, where
U
is taken to have degree
-2
, by letting
(\mathbb{H}_{k})_{-2n}=\mathbb{H}_{k,-2n}:=H_{k}(S_{n},\mathbb{Z})
for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and using the action
U:H_{k}(S_{n},\mathbb{Z})\to H_{k}(S_{n+1},\mathbb{Z})
induced by the natural inclusions. (Although taking
-2n
instead of
n
looks unnatural, this choice is motivated by the compatibility of the lattice homology theory with the Heegaard Floer theory and similarities with several other theories of low-dimensional topology—see, e.g.,
[
29
,
49
,
55
]
.)

We may also consider the module
\mathbb{H}_{*}:=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{H}_{k}
as a whole, which then has the structure of a
(\mathbb{N}\times 2\mathbb{Z})
-graded
\mathbb{Z}[U]
-module with
U
of bidegree
(0,-2)
; the first grading is the usual
homological grading
, and the second, which we call the
doubleweight grading
, is induced by the one above.

From the constructions, we have

(5.1.3)

\min w_{0}=\min_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}w_{0}(\ell)=\min\{n\in\mathbb{Z}\mid\mathbb{H}_{0,-2n}\neq 0\}=\min\{n\in\mathbb{Z}\mid\mathbb{H}_{*,-2n}\neq 0\}.

Using the definition of the Euler characteristic
\eu(\mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o))
formulated in
[
3
,
4
,
36
]
, we find

(5.1.4)

\delta(C,o)=\eu(\mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o));

that is, the lattice homology categorifies the delta invariant.

The following result allows us to compute
\mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o)
using only a
finite
cubical complex:

Theorem 5.1.5

(
[
3
, Lemma 4.2.1]
,
[
4
, Proposition 1.2.17]
,
[
36
, Theorem 2.3.2]
)
.

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
, define the rectangle
R(0,\ell):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\mid 0\leq x\leq\ell\}
. Then, for any lattice point
\ell\geq c
, the inclusion
S_{n}\cap R(0,\ell)\hookrightarrow S_{n}
is a homotopy equivalence for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular,
S_{n}
is empty for
n\ll 0
, as noted above, and contractible for
n\geq\max w_{0}|_{R(0,c)}
.

Therefore, for any lattice point
\ell\geq c
, there exists a canonical bigraded
\mathbb{Z}[U]
-module isomorphism
\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{*}\big(S_{n}\cap R(0,\ell),\mathbb{Z}\big)\to\mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o)
.

5.2.
The level filtration on lattice homology

The lattice homology of our curve germ
(C,o)
arises from the filtration of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{r}
by the spaces
S_{n}
; given a second filtration of the same cubical complex, we can consider the induced filtrations on the
S_{n}
to obtain a corresponding spectral sequence supported by the lattice homology, from which we produce finer numerical invariants of
(C,o)
. Here we recall the basics of this approach—for the full details, see
[
49
]
.

Definition 5.2.1
.

(a)

The
level filtration on the cubical complex
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{r}
of
(C,o)
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-indexed increasing filtration
\cdots\subseteq\mathfrak{X}_{-2}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}_{-1}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}_{0}\subseteq\cdots
given by letting
\mathfrak{X}_{d}
be the full subcomplex of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{r}
spanned by the vertices
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
such that
|\ell|\geq-d
for each
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, this means that
\mathfrak{X}_{d}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{r}
for all
d\geq 0
; hence we typically restrict ourselves to the subspaces
\{\mathfrak{X}_{-d}\}_{d\geq 0}
.

(b)

The
level filtration on the lattice homology
\mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o)
of
(C,o)
is the increasing (
-\mathbb{N}
)-indexed filtration
F_{*}\mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o)
of bigraded
\mathbb{Z}[U]
-modules given by setting

F_{-d}\mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o):=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\im(H_{*}(S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-d},\mathbb{Z})\to H_{*}(S_{n},\mathbb{Z}))

for each
d\in\mathbb{N}
, where the maps in homology are those induced by the inclusions
S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-d}\hookrightarrow S_{n}
and the
U
-action is given as before by the inclusions among the
S_{n}
.

(c)

The
spectral sequence of
(C,o)
associated to the level filtration
is the homological spectral sequence of (singly-)graded
\mathbb{Z}[U]
-modules given by the direct sum of the spectral sequences induced by the filtrations
\{S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-d}\}_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}
of the spaces
S_{n}
; the first page is then given by

E_{-d,q}^{1}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{-d+q}(S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-d},S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-d-1},\mathbb{Z}).

This converges to the associated graded module of the level filtration on the lattice homology of
(C,o)
; that is,

E_{-d,q}^{k}\Rightarrow E_{-d,q}^{\infty}=\gr_{-d}^{F}\mathbb{H}_{-d+q}(C,o).

The graded pieces
(E^{1}_{*,*})_{-2n}
of the
E^{1}
-page under the doubleweight grading will be particularly important in the next discussions. In fact, they admit a refinement (defined only at the
E^{1}
-page level and not as part of the spectral sequence as a whole)—if we let
\mathfrak{Y}_{-\ell}
be the full subcomplex of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{r}
spanned by the vertices
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
such that
\ell^{\prime}\geq\ell
, we have the following:

Proposition 5.2.2

(
[
49
]
)
.

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
and
q,n\in\mathbb{Z}
, set

E_{-\ell,q}^{1}:=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{-|\ell|+q}(S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{-\ell},S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{-\ell}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-|\ell|-1})

with the usual
\mathbb{Z}[U]
-module structure. Then we have a direct sum decomposition

E_{-d,q}^{1}=\bigoplus_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r},\,|\ell|=d}E_{-\ell,q}^{1}

of (singly-)graded
\mathbb{Z}[U]
-modules.

We emphasize that all the embedded cubical subcomplexes
S_{n}
, and hence all the entries of all spectral sequences, are well-defined invariants of reduced curve singularities. In this note we will focus mainly on the minimal value of the weight function
w_{0}
, which is already visible at the level of the ordinary lattice homology, and on the nonvanishing properties of
(E_{-d,q}^{1})_{-2n}
, which require some of this additional information.

Remark 5.2.3
.

Let
\pi:(C^{\prime},o^{\prime})\to(C,o)
be a finite birational map of reduced complex-analytic curve germs. Then Lemma
3.2.2
implies that, for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a naturally-induced inclusion
S_{n}^{C^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow S_{n}^{C}
of the weight-
(\leq\!n)
spaces which moreover respects the level filtration. These inclusions thus functorially induce graded
\mathbb{Z}[U]
-module morphisms of the corresponding lattice homology groups
\mathbb{H}_{*}(-)
, of the filtered pieces
F_{*}\mathbb{H}_{*}(-)
, and of the spectral sequence entries
E_{*,*}^{k}
. That is, all these homological invariants define covariant functors on the category of reduced complex-analytic curve germs with finite birational maps.

In fact, all the above facts also hold for the inclusions of subcurves
(C_{J},o)\subseteq(C,o)
(see Subsection
2.4
); the identification of
\mathbb{N}^{|J|}
with a sublattice of
\mathbb{N}^{r}
gives a natural inclusion
S_{n}^{C_{J}}\hookrightarrow S_{n}^{C}
respecting the level filtration for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, whence we obtain the functoriality of our homological invariants as before. Indeed, by combining this observation with the previous one, we find that the invariants define functors on the category of reduced complex-analytic curve germs with finite maps which do not have degree larger than one over any component.

5.3.
Minimal Spectral Cycles

As mentioned, we will be interested in the non-vanishing of concrete graded summands
(E^{1}_{-d,q})_{-2n}
; as we will see in Sections
8
and
10
, such non-vanishing properties can be used to characterize and separate
different families of singularities. Continuing with the conventions of Section
2
, we begin by noting that the non-vanishing of individual summands in the direct sum decomposition of Proposition
5.2.2
imposes constraints on the behavior of the weight function
w_{0}
:

Lemma 5.3.1
.

Fix
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
and
q,n\in\mathbb{Z}
. If the direct summand
(E_{-\ell,q}^{1})_{-2n}
is nonzero, then
-|\ell|+q\geq 0
and there exist indices
1\leq i_{0}<i_{1}<\ldots<i_{-|\ell|+q}\leq r
such that, for each subset
I\subseteq\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{-|\ell|+q}\}
,
w_{0}\left(\ell+\sum_{i\in I}e^{i}\right)=n+|\ell|-q+|I|
.

Proof.

Let
B=R(\ell,\ell+e)\subset{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{r}
, and define
A=B\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-|\ell|-1}
to be the subcomplex of
B
spanned by all its vertices other than
\ell
itself. Then, by excision and deformation retraction of pairs,
(E_{-\ell,q}^{1})_{-2n}=H_{-|\ell|+q}(S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{-\ell},S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{-\ell}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-|\ell|-1})\cong H_{-|\ell|+q}(S_{n}\cap B,S_{n}\cap A)
.

This relative homology group consists of
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations of
(-|\ell|+q)
-dimensional lower faces of
B
with boundary contained in
A
which are moreover contained in
S_{n}
, modulo those which can be realized as the boundaries of such combinations of
(-|\ell|+q+1)
-dimensional lower faces contained in
S_{n}
. As such, it is generated for
|\ell|+q>0
by the
(-|\ell|+q+1)
-dimensional cubes rooted at
\ell
such that, for each, all lower faces are contained in
S_{n}
but the interior is not. (To see this formally, use the isomorphism
H_{-|\ell|+q}(S_{n}\cap B,S_{n}\cap A)\cong\tilde{H}_{-|\ell|+q}((S_{n}\cap B)/(S_{n}\cap A))
, identify the quotient space with the suspension of the appropriate simplicial complex on a subset of the vertices
\ell+e^{i}
for
1\leq i\leq r
, and apply the long exact sequence in relative homology.) In particular, for this homology group to be nonzero as hypothesized, it is necessary that at least one such cube exist—in the
|\ell|+q=0
case, it is clear that all 1-dimensional cubes rooted at
\ell
must be of this form.

We can identify each such cube with the indices
1\leq i_{0}<i_{1}<\ldots<i_{-|\ell|+q}\leq r
such that the vertices of the cube are given by
\ell+\sum_{i\in I}e^{i}
for all subsets
I\subseteq\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{-|\ell|+q}\}
; on the level of vertices, our condition on containments and non-containments in
S_{n}
then becomes the requirement that
w_{0}^{C}\left(\ell+\sum_{i\in I}e^{i}\right)\leq n
when
I
is a proper subset of
\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{-|\ell|+q}\}
and
w_{0}^{C}\left(\ell+\sum_{i\in I}e^{i}\right)>n
for
I=\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{-|\ell|+q}\}
. Hence, by (
2.2.4
), our top vertex must have weight exactly
n+1
and those on the next level down, which correspond to subsets satisfying
|I|=-|\ell|+q
, must have weight exactly
n
. Then a downward induction on the levels of our cube, together with (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.8
), proves that our weight requirement for the lower faces of the cube is equivalent to the equality
w_{0}^{C}\left(\ell+\sum_{i\in I}e^{i}\right)=n+|\ell|-q+|I|
holding for all subsets
I\subseteq\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{-|\ell|+q}\}
.
∎

Thus we obtain a fairly explicit description of the substructures which must appear in our weighted lattice in order for particular terms of the first page of the spectral sequence to be nonzero. In particular, if we wish to understand where such nonzero terms can appear, it will often suffice to study the behavior of the weight function
w_{0}
directly. As a coarse constraint, (
2.2.4
) now imposes clear bounds on the allowable indices for nonzero terms in this
E^{1}
-page; by taking into account the multiplicity vector
m
of
(C,o)
, we can sharpen these for certain indices which behave well with respect to multiples of this vector’s weight
2-|m|
(although more general statements are also possible in theory—see Remark
5.3.4
(b)):

Lemma 5.3.2
.

Suppose
|m|\geq 3
. For any
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
(E_{-d,k+d}^{1})_{-2((2-|m|)j+k)}=0
for each integer
d<j|m|
and
(E_{-\ell,k+|m|j}^{1})_{-2((2-|m|)j+k)}=0
for each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
with
|\ell|=j|m|
but
\ell\neq jm
.

Proof.

Fix some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
such that
|\ell|\leq j|m|
but
\ell\neq jm
; by Lemma
5.3.1
, it suffices to show that
w_{0}(\ell)\neq(2-|m|)j
.

Let
\tilde{\jmath}\in\mathbb{N}
be minimal such that
\ell\leq\tilde{\jmath}m
and consider any increasing path
\{x_{s}\}_{s=0}^{|\ell|}
in
\mathbb{N}^{r}
such that
x_{0}=0
,
x_{s}=x_{s-1}+e^{i(s)}
for some
1\leq i(s)\leq r
when
1\leq s\leq|\ell|
, and
x_{|\ell|}=\ell
; note that, by (
2.2.4
),
w_{0}(x_{s})-w_{0}(x_{s-1})=\pm 1
for any
1\leq s\leq|\ell|
, and hence, if we let
\upsilon\geq 0
be the number of such values
s
where this difference is positive,
w_{0}(\ell)=2\upsilon-|\ell|
. Indeed, (
2.2.4
) and the fact that the lattice points
\{am\}_{a\in\mathbb{N}}
are semigroup elements imply that, for any such
s
where
x_{s-1}\leq am
but
x_{s}\not\leq am
for some
a\in\mathbb{N}
, this difference is
+1
; the minimality of
\tilde{\jmath}
then implies that
\upsilon\geq\tilde{\jmath}
and so
w_{0}(\ell)\geq 2\tilde{\jmath}-|\ell|
. Thus it now suffices to show that
2\tilde{\jmath}-|\ell|>(2-|m|)j
, or
j|m|-|\ell|>2(j-\tilde{\jmath})
.

If
\tilde{\jmath}>j
—that is, if
\ell\not\leq jm
—then this is immediate from the hypothesis
|\ell|\leq j|m|
. On the other hand, if
\tilde{\jmath}<j
—which entails that
\ell\leq\tilde{\jmath}m<jm
—we have
j|m|-|\ell|\geq j|m|-\tilde{\jmath}|m|=|m|(j-\tilde{\jmath})
, and the result now follows by the hypothesis
|m|\geq 3>2
. Finally, we note that, if
\tilde{\jmath}=j
, the relation
\ell\leq jm
and hypothesis
\ell\neq jm
guarantee that
|\ell|<j|m|
and so
j|m|-|\ell|>j|m|-j|m|=0=2(j-\tilde{\jmath})
.
∎

Hence we can see when
|m|\geq 3
that, for any fixed homological degree
k
and weight
n
such that
n=(2-|m|)j+k
for some
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
d=j|m|
is the least natural number such that
(E_{-d,k+d}^{1})_{-2n}
can possibly be nonzero, and in this case we have
(E_{-d,k+d}^{1})_{-2n}=(E_{-jm,k+d}^{1})_{-2n}
. Elements of this relative homology group are thus interesting enough to warrant a name:

Definition 5.3.3
.

Suppose again that
|m|\geq 3
. Then, for any
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
and
n=(2-|m|)j+k
, we define the
group of minimal spectral
k
-cycles of weight
n
for
(C,o)
by
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}=\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}(C,o):=(E_{-j|m|,k+j|m|}^{1})_{-2n}=(E_{-jm,k+j|m|}^{1})_{-2n}
. In a slight abuse of terminology, we will say that
(C,o)

has a minimal spectral
k
-cycle of weight
n
if
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}(C,o)\neq 0
.

Remark 5.3.4
.

Here some comments are in order.

(a)

The terms
(E_{-d,k+d}^{1})_{-2n}
in the
E^{1}
-page, for
d\in\mathbb{Z}
and
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
, measure relative homological degree-
k
features of successive filtered pieces of the space
S_{n}
under the level filtration at the
d
th level of
\mathbb{N}^{r}
. Hence, as mentioned above,
(C,o)
has a minimal spectral
k
-cycle of weight
(2-|m|)j+k
exactly when such relative homological-degree-
k
features of
S_{(2-|m|)j+k}
appear at the lowest level of
\mathbb{N}^{r}
where they possibly can, according to the bound established in Lemma
5.3.2
; this is the motivation for the term “minimal”. (Note that this minimality is relative to the fixed multiplicity
|m|
.) We call our cycles “spectral” to emphasize that they appear in the
E^{1}
-page of our spectral sequence rather than in the ordinary lattice homology groups of
(C,o)
. (E.g., for
D_{n}
,
n\geq 5
odd,
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}=(E^{1}_{-3,4})_{0}\not=0
but
\mathbb{H}_{1}=0
.)

(b)

Our definition is not as broad as possible; specifically, one could meaningfully give a more general notion of the group
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}(C,o)
of minimal spectral cycles of
any
weight
n
, not just one of the form
(2-|m|)j+k
for some
j\in\mathbb{N}
. However, dealing with these weights would require a more general version of Lemma
5.3.2
, correspondingly more difficult to state and prove; since the given definition encompasses all cases relevant to our present purposes, we omit these considerations.

(c)

The statements of Lemmas
5.3.1
and
5.3.2
are compatible with the general vanishing (
11.1.2
); in fact, Lemma
5.3.1
provides a new proof of this vanishing.

Examples 5.3.5
.

In fact, the following are all the cases which will appear in our classifications and characterizations:

•

If
|m|=3
, then
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)=(E_{-3,4}^{1})_{0}=(E_{-m,1+|m|}^{1})_{-2(0)}
. (Here
j=1
.)

•

If
|m|=3
, then
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)=(E_{-6,7}^{1})_{2}=(E_{-2m,1+2|m|}^{1})_{-2(-1)}
. (Here
j=2
.)

•

If
|m|=4
, then
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)=(E_{-4,5}^{1})_{2}=(E_{-m,1+|m|}^{1})_{-2(-1)}
. (Here
j=1
.)

(Indeed, under Definition
5.3.3
, these are
all
the possible cases where it makes sense to discuss minimal filtered 1-cycles of weight either 0 or
-1
for
(C,o)
, as we see by finding all natural number solutions
j,|m|\in\mathbb{N}
to the equations
(2-|m|)j+1=0
and
(2-|m|)j+1=-1
respectively; however, the constraints on
|m|
are in some sense artificial, and adopting a more general definition along the lines suggested in Remark
5.3.4
(b) would give us definitions of
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)
and
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)
for any
|m|\geq 3
.)

We observe a consequence of the existence of minimal spectral cycles on the shape of the semigroup:

Lemma 5.3.6
.

As before, take
|m|\geq 3
,
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
n=(2-|m|)j+k
. Then, if
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}(C,o)\neq 0
, we have
\mathscr{S}_{C}\setminus\{0,m,\ldots,(j-1)m\}\subseteq jm+\mathbb{N}^{r}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
5.3.2
, we consider sequences of lattice points with successive differences in
\{e^{1},\ldots,e^{r}\}
which start at
0
and end at
jm
; in particular, we will restrict our attention to those which pass through all of the intervening multiples
0,m,\ldots,jm
of the multiplicity vector. By (
2.2.4
), the difference in weights between any two successive lattice points along such a path is
\pm 1
; since
0,m,\ldots,(j-1)m\in\mathscr{S}_{C}
, we know that at least
j
of these steps give increases in weight, so
w_{0}^{C}(jm)\geq j-(j|m|-j)=(2-|m|)j
, with a failure of equality if any step from a point other than
0,m,\ldots,(j-1)m
increases the weight. In particular, Lemma
5.3.1
tells us that this cannot occur if
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}(C,o)\neq 0
.

Suppose we have
\ell=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r})\in\mathscr{S}_{C}\setminus\{0,m,\ldots,(j-1)m\}
which is not contained in
jm+\mathbb{N}^{r}
, and let
0\leq\tilde{\jmath}<j
be the greatest natural number such that
\tilde{\jmath}m\leq\ell
. We will produce a path from
\tilde{\jmath}m
to
(\tilde{\jmath}+1)m
which has at least one weight increase after the first step; since we can concatenate this with other paths between successive multiples of
m
to produce a path of the sort discussed above, this will preclude the possibility that
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}(C,o)\neq 0
.

Let
\ell^{\prime}:=\min\{\ell,(\tilde{\jmath}+1)m\}
and note by the definition of
\tilde{\jmath}
that
\ell^{\prime}\neq(\tilde{\jmath}+1)m
. Moreover, since
\tilde{\jmath}m+e^{i}\leq(\tilde{\jmath}+1)m
for all
1\leq i\leq r
, the hypothesis that
\ell\neq\tilde{\jmath}m
and so
\tilde{\jmath}m<\ell
gives us
\ell^{\prime}>\tilde{\jmath}m
as well. If we take any path from
\tilde{\jmath}m
to
(\tilde{\jmath}+1)m
which passes through
\ell^{\prime}
, using (
2.2.4
) with the semigroup element
\ell
then reveals that the weight will increase in the step from
\ell^{\prime}
to the next point. The result follows.
∎

We note as well that, by the discussion of the generators of each group
(E_{-\ell,q}^{1})_{-2n}
in the proof of Lemma
5.3.1
and the fact that each
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}(C,o)
is given by
(E_{-jm,k+j|m|}^{1})_{-2n}
, each group
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}
for
k>0
will be generated by some subset of
\binom{r}{k+1}
elements, the lower
(k+1)
-dimensional faces of the
r
-dimensional cube, with fixed relations among these arising from the lower
(k+2)
-dimensional faces; by continuing this reasoning, we find that
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}
is a subgroup of an abelian group of rank
\binom{r}{k+1}-\binom{r}{k+2}+\binom{r}{k+3}-\cdots+(-1)^{r-k+1}\binom{r}{r}=\binom{r-1}{k}
. (If
k=0
, it is immediate that the rank of
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}
is bounded above by
\binom{r-1}{k}=1
.) Therefore, since
r\leq|m|
, we define:

Definition 5.3.7
.

Suppose
|m|\geq 3
, choose
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
, and set
n=(2-|m|)j+k
. Then we say that the group
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}(C,o)
of minimal spectral
k
-cycles of weight
n

has maximal rank
when
\rk\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}(C,o)=\binom{|m|-1}{k}
.

As with the definition of
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}(C,o)
itself, we consider questions of minimality and maximality relative to the fixed multiplicity
|m|
—here the number of components is allowed to vary, and hence in particular a curve where any
\mathfrak{M}_{k,n}(C,o)
for
k>0
has maximal rank will satisfy
r=|m|
.

Examples 5.3.8
.

For our purposes, the relevant cases will be as follows:

•

If
|m|=3
, then
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)
has maximal rank if and only if
\rk\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)=\rk(E_{-6,7}^{1})_{2}=2
; note that this implies
r=|m|=3
and hence
m=(1,1,1)
.

•

If
|m|=4
, then
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)
has maximal rank if and only if
\rk\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)=\rk(E_{-4,5}^{1})_{2}=3
; this implies
r=|m|=4
and hence
m=(1,1,1,1)
.

6.
Computations for the foundational examples

In this section, we present the
w_{0}
-values of most of the
ADE
and
T_{pq}
plane curve germs, both to give the reader examples to work with and to establish some results for our own later use. In particular, we will be interested in the minimal weight values for these germs, which will turn out to distinguish the
A
-germs,
DE
-germs, and
T_{pq}
germs from one another, and in the behaviors of the appropriate groups of minimal spectral 1-cycles (a key novelty of the present note—see the preceding Subsection
5.3
), which distinguish, e.g.,
D
-germs from
E
-germs and encode various other important properties.

6.1.
Formulae for
\mathfrak{h}
and
w_{0}

For our purposes, it will often be enough to know the weight values for certain key lattice points, rather than the full lattice; nevertheless, for the reader’s convenience, we give them for points within the conductor rectangle
R(0,c)
, from which the weights for the full lattice can be recovered easily by (
2.2.4
) and the inclusion
c+\mathbb{N}^{r}\subseteq\mathscr{S}
. In particular, from (
2.2.4
) or Theorem
5.1.5
, we can conclude that
\min w_{0}=\min w_{0}|_{R(0,c)}
. Indeed, using the symmetry (
2.3.2
), we can see that we need only fill in the
w_{0}
-values of ‘half’ the points in
R(0,c)
.

Per Definition
2.1.3
, the
w_{0}
-values can be computed from the Hilbert function
\mathfrak{h}
; these can be obtained from (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.3
) respectively if we know the semigroup of values
\mathscr{S}
. Otherwise, we can find the Hilbert function using the following general formula, which will be our typical approach for
r\geq 2
. Recall that the Poincaré series (see, e.g.,
[
29
]
)
P({\bf t})=P_{C}({\bf t})=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}\mathfrak{p}(\ell){\bf t}^{\ell}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}\mathfrak{p}(\ell){t_{1}}^{\ell_{1}}\cdots{t_{r}}^{\ell_{r}}
of a curve germ
(C,o)
is determined by the germ’s Hilbert function according to the formula

(6.1.1)

\mathfrak{p}(\ell):=\sum_{J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}}(-1)^{|J|+1}\mathfrak{h}(\ell+e^{J}),\quad\text{where }e^{J}:=\sum_{j\in J}e^{j}.

For arbitrary reduced complex-analytic curve germs, it can be the case that different Hilbert series
H({\bf t})=H_{C}({\bf t}):=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}\mathfrak{h}(\ell){\bf t}^{\ell}
provide identical Poincaré series (see, e.g.,
[
15
]
). Hence, in general, the Hilbert function cannot be recovered from
P_{C}
. However, if we consider the Poincaré series of all subgerms
\{(C_{J},o)\}_{J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}}

(cf. Subsection
2.4
), then this is possible:

Theorem 6.1.2

(
[
29
, Theorem 3.4.3]
; see also
[
43
, Corollary 4.3]
)
.

With the above notations,

H_{C}({\bf t})=\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-t_{i})}\sum_{\emptyset\neq J=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{|J|}\}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}}(-1)^{|J|-1}t_{i_{1}}\cdots t_{i_{|J|}}P_{C_{J}}(t_{i_{1}},\ldots,t_{i_{|J|}}).

Example 6.1.3
.

If
r=1
, then (writing
t
for
t_{1}
) we have
H(t)=tP(t)/(1-t)
. If
r=2
, then

H_{C}(t_{1},t_{2})=\frac{1}{(1-t_{1})(1-t_{2})}\Big(t_{1}P_{C_{1}}(t_{1})+t_{2}P_{C_{2}}(t_{2})-t_{1}t_{2}P_{C}(t_{1},t_{2})\Big).

Hence, if we write
e=(1,1)
as in Section
2
, we have, for any
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathfrak{h}^{C}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})=\mathfrak{h}^{C_{1}}(\ell_{1})+\mathfrak{h}^{C_{2}}(\ell_{2})-\#\{p\in\supp P_{C}\mid\ell\geq p+e\},

since
P_{C}(t_{1},t_{2})=\sum_{(p_{1},p_{2})\in\supp P_{C}}{t_{1}}^{p_{1}}{t_{2}}^{p_{2}}
. Thus, for
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,

w_{0}^{C}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})=w_{0}^{C_{1}}(\ell_{1})+w_{0}^{C_{2}}(\ell_{2})-2\cdot\#\{p\in\supp P_{C}\mid\ell\geq p+e\}.

On the other hand, for a reduced complex-analytic
plane
curve germ, as we study in this section, the Poincaré series is determined by the multivariable Alexander polynomial
\Delta({\bf t})
of the link of

(C,o)\subset(\mathbb{C}^{2},o)
. Indeed,
P(t)(1-t)=\Delta(t)
if
r=1
and
P({\bf t})=\Delta({\bf t})
for
r>1
(
[
13
,
14
]
); in particular, this implies that the series
P({\bf t})
is a polynomial for
r>1
. Using combinatorial formulae for Alexander polynomials in terms of the embedded resolution graphs (or splice diagrams) of plane curve singularities (
[
25
, Section 12]
), we can now compute the Poincaré series of all subgerms; this is how we obtain the Hilbert functions
\mathfrak{h}
for the curves considered in this section, as well as, if we wish, the semigroups
\mathscr{S}
by a further application of (
2.2.2
).

It follows from our discussion here that the Hilbert function of a reduced plane curve germ is an invariant of the embedded topological type, and hence the weight function
w_{0}
is as well. For completeness, we note for such germs that the multivariable Alexander polynomial—and hence likewise the multivariable Poincaré series—is a
complete
invariant for the embedded topological type (
[
53
]
); in particular,
H_{C}({\bf t})
is determined by
P_{C}({\bf t})
alone in this case, although we do not give an explicit formula.

We now list the results of the weight computations for the
ADE
and
T_{pq}
germs
(C,o)
, using the notation of Section
2
; since these are hypersurface germs, we also adopt the convention that
f
denotes the defining equation (in terms of coordinates
x
and
y
).

6.2.
Invariants of germs
A_{n}
,
n\geq 0
even

Here
f=x^{n+1}+y^{2}
is irreducible and so
r=1
. Moreover, we can compute
c=n
,
\delta=n/2
, and
\mathscr{S}=\langle 2,n+1\rangle
. The function
w_{0}^{C}
is then given by the following, where we
bold
the values of semigroup elements and
box
the conductor:

\small\begin{array}[]{c|ccccccccccc}\ell&0&1&2&3&4&\cdots&n-1&n&n+1&n+2&\cdots\\
\hline\cr w_{0}&\bf 0&1&\bf 0&1&\bf 0&\cdots&1&\framebox{\bf 0}&\bf 1&\bf 2&\cdots\end{array}

6.3.
Invariants of germs
A_{n}
,
n=2k-1\geq 1

Here
f=x^{n+1}+y^{2}=x^{2k}+y^{2}=(x^{k}+iy)(x^{k}-iy)
, so
r=2
. We use the formulae from Example
6.1.3
; by
[
25
, Section 12]
,
P_{i}(t_{i})=1/(1-t_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
(since
(C_{i},o)
is smooth) and
P_{C}(t_{1},t_{2})=1+t_{1}t_{2}+\cdots+(t_{1}t_{2})^{k-1}
. Hence, for
\ell\in R(0,c)
, we have
w_{0}^{C}(\ell)=|\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}|
, where
c=(k,k)
. We illustrate this concretely by giving the
w_{0}^{C}(\ell)
-values for
\ell\in R(0,(3,3))
in the cases
k=1
and
k=2
:

\footnotesize\phantom{+}{}^{k\,=\,1}\ \begin{array}[]{c|cccc}&3&\bf 2&\bf 3&\bf 4\\
&2&\bf 1&\bf 2&\bf 3\\
&1&\framebox{\bf 0}&\bf 1&\bf 2\\
&\bf 0&1&2&3\\
\hline\cr\rule{0.0pt}{10.79993pt}\!\!\!{\phantom{|}{}^{\ell_{2}}}_{\ell_{1}}&&&&\end{array}\hskip 68.99895pt\phantom{+}^{k\,=\,2}\ \begin{array}[]{c|cccc}&3&2&\bf 1&\bf 2\\
&2&1&\framebox{\bf 0}&\bf 1\\
&1&\bf 0&1&2\\
&\bf 0&1&2&3\\
\hline\cr\rule{0.0pt}{10.79993pt}\!\!\!{\phantom{|}{}^{\ell_{2}}}_{\ell_{1}}&&&&\end{array}

6.4.
Invariants of germs
D_{n}
,
n\geq 5
odd

In this case,
f=(x^{2}-y^{n-2})y
,
r=2
,
m=(2,1)
,
c=(n-1,2)
, and
\delta=(n+1)/2
; the Poincaré series are
P_{C_{1}}(t_{1})=(1+{t_{1}}^{n-2})/(1-{t_{1}}^{2})
,
P_{C_{2}}(t_{2})=1/(1-t_{2})
, and
P_{C}(t_{1},t_{2})=1+{t_{1}}^{n-2}t_{2}
. Then the weights
w_{0}^{C}(\ell)
for
\ell
in
R(0,c)
(left-hand side) and the weight-at-most-
0
space
S_{0}^{C}
(right-hand side) are as follows:

\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c|ccccccccc}&2&1&\bf 0&1&\bf 0&\cdots&\bf 0&1&\framebox{\bf 0}\\
&1&0&\bf-1&0&\bf-1&\cdots&\bf-1&\bf 0&1\\
&\bf 0&1&0&1&0&\cdots&0&1&2\\
\hline\cr\rule{0.0pt}{10.79993pt}\!\!\!{\phantom{|}{}^{\ell_{2}}}_{\ell_{1}}&0&1&2&3&4&\cdots&&&\!\!\!n-1\end{array}\hskip 51.7479pt\begin{picture}(0.0,15.0)\put(80.0,15.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$\ldots$}}\put(0.0,15.0){\line{1}{0}{65.0}}\put(15.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{20.0}}\put(45.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{20.0}}\put(-15.0,5.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(0.0,15.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(15.0,15.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(30.0,15.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(45.0,15.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(60.0,15.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(15.0,25.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(15.0,5.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(45.0,25.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(45.0,5.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(95.0,15.0){\line{1}{0}{65.0}}\put(115.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{20.0}}\put(145.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{20.0}}\par\put(175.0,25.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(100.0,15.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(115.0,15.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(130.0,15.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(145.0,15.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(160.0,15.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(115.0,25.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(115.0,5.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(145.0,25.0){\circle*{3.0}}\put(145.0,5.0){\circle*{3.0}}\par{{}\raise 25.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{{

\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip-0.09999pt\hskip-0.2pt\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.4pt\hss}\raise-0.16666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.15001pt\hskip-0.2pt\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.4pt\hss}\raise-0.33331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.40001pt\hskip-0.2pt\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.4pt\hss}\ignorespaces}}\hss}\ignorespaces}{

{

The dashed lines represent levels
d
where
(E^{1}_{-d,1+d})_{0}\neq 0
. The ‘thick line wedge’ represents a
minimal
spectral 1-cycle of weight 0 generating
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}=(E^{1}_{-3,4})_{0}=H_{1}(S_{0}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-3},S_{0}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-4})=\mathbb{Z}
(corresponding to
d=3
).

6.5.
Invariants of germs
D_{n}
,
n=2k\geq 4

In this case,
f=(x^{2}-y^{n-2})y=(x-y^{k-1})(x+y^{k-1})y
,
r=3
,
m=(1,1,1)
,
c=(k,k,2)
. The Poincaré series are
P_{C_{i}}(t_{i})=1/(1-t_{i})
for every
i\in\{1,2,3\}
,
P_{C_{\{1,3\}}}=P_{C_{\{2,3\}}}=1
,
P_{C_{\{1,2\}}}(t_{1},t_{2})=1+t_{1}t_{2}+\cdots+(t_{1}t_{2})^{k-2}
, and
P_{C}(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})=1-{t_{1}}^{k-1}{t_{2}}^{k-1}t_{3}
.

If
n=4
, then the
w_{0}^{C}
-table in
R(0,(2,2,2))=R(0,c)
and the space
S_{0}
are the following (where the dashed lines now indicate only relative positioning in three-dimensional space):

\par\put(190.0,15.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){$(E^{1}_{-3,4})_{0}=\mathbb{Z}^{2}$}}\end{picture}

For
n>4
, the
w_{0}
-values in
R(0,c)
are as follows. For
\ell_{3}=0
,
w_{0}(\ell)=|\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}|
for any
0\leq\ell<(k,k)
, while
w_{0}(k,k,0)=2
. The values for
\ell_{3}=2
can be obtained from the values with
\ell_{3}=0
since
w_{0}
is symmetric in
R(0,c)
; that is,
w_{0}(\ell)=w_{0}(c-\ell)
per (
2.3.2
). Finally, for
\ell_{3}=1
, we have
w_{0}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},1)=w_{0}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},0)+\epsilon
, where
\epsilon=1
for
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})=(0,0)
and
\epsilon=-1
otherwise.

Since we will later need some concrete values in the rectangle
R(0,(2,2,2))
, we provide them here together with
S_{0}\cap R(0,(2,2,2))
for the convenience of the reader.

\footnotesize\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}&2&1&0\\
&1&0&1\\
&\bf 0&1&2\\
\hline\cr\rule{0.0pt}{10.79993pt}\!\!\!{\phantom{|}{}^{\ell_{2}}}_{\ell_{1}}&&&\end{array}\\[1.0pt]
\ell_{3}\,=\,0\end{subarray}\hskip 17.25105pt\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}&1&0&\bf-1\\
&0&\bf-1&0\\
&1&0&1\\
\hline\cr\rule{0.0pt}{10.79993pt}\!\!\!{\phantom{|}{}^{\ell_{2}}}_{\ell_{1}}&&&\end{array}\\[1.0pt]
\ell_{3}\,=\,1\end{subarray}\hskip 17.25105pt\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}&2&1&\bf 0\\
&1&\bf 0&1\\
&2&1&2\\
\hline\cr\rule{0.0pt}{10.79993pt}\!\!\!{\phantom{|}{}^{\ell_{2}}}_{\ell_{1}}&&&\end{array}\\[1.0pt]
\ell_{3}\,=\,2\end{subarray}\hskip 51.7479pt\begin{picture}(100.0,15.0){{}\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 100.0pt\hbox{\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.60605pt}\hss}\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 101.93939pt\hbox{\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.60605pt}\hss}\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 103.87878pt\hbox{\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.60605pt}\hss}\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 105.81818pt\hbox{\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.60605pt}\hss}\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 107.75757pt\hbox{\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.60605pt}\hss}\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 109.69696pt\hbox{\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.60605pt}\hss}\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 111.63635pt\hbox{\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.60605pt}\hss}\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 113.57574pt\hbox{\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.60605pt}\hss}\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 115.51514pt\hbox{\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.60605pt}\hss}\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 117.45453pt\hbox{\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.60605pt}\hss}\raise 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 119.39392pt\hbox{\vrule height=0.2pt,depth=0.2pt,width=0.60605pt}\hss}\ignorespaces}{

Note that the
D_{4}
is distinguished from the
D_{n}
singularities for all
n\geq 5
by the rank of
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}=(E_{-3,4}^{1})_{0}
.

6.6.
Invariants of germs
E_{n}
,
n\in\{6,7,8\}

The
E_{6}
and
E_{8}
cases, where
r=1
, can be done easily, similarly to the case
A_{2k}
of Subsection
6.2
, using
\mathscr{S}^{E_{6}}=\langle 3,4\rangle
and
\mathscr{S}^{E_{8}}=\langle 3,5\rangle
:

\small\phantom{+}{}^{E_{6}}\ \begin{array}[]{c|ccccccc}\ell&0&1&2&3&4&5&6\\
\hline\cr w_{0}&\bf 0&1&0&\bf-1&\bf 0&1&\framebox{\bf 0}\end{array}\hskip 34.5021pt\phantom{+}^{E_{8}}\ \begin{array}[]{c|ccccccccc}\ell&0&1&2&3&4&5&6&7&8\\
\hline\cr w_{0}&\bf 0&1&0&\bf-1&0&\bf-1&\bf 0&1&\framebox{\bf 0}\end{array}

For
E_{7}
,
f=(x^{2}+y^{3})x
,
r=2
,
m=(2,1)
,
c=(5,3)
, and
\delta=4
. The values of
w_{0}^{E_{7}}
in
R(0,c)
and the space
S_{0}
are the following:

\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{c|cccccc}&3&2&1&\bf 0&1&\framebox{\bf 0}\\
&2&1&0&\bf-1&\bf 0&1\\
&1&0&\bf-1&0&1&2\\
&\bf 0&1&0&1&2&3\\
