and so, in particular, it is nonzero for
ab>2
.

6.11.
Minimum weight for parabolic and hyperbolic germs

So far, we have given some example weight computations for
T_{pq}
germs, from which we can see that
\min w_{0}=-2
in the cases considered. This fact will be our main takeaway from these computations in the sequel and so, in lieu of giving an exhaustive survey of the
T_{pq}
family, we here prove that the corresponding lower bound holds in general; equality will follow by Theorem
1
and the fact that the
T_{pq}
germs are of infinite Cohen-Macaulay type.

Proposition 6.11.1
.

Let
p\leq q
be integers such that
1/p+1/q\leq 1/2
. Then
\min w_{0}^{T_{pq}}\geq-2
.

Proof.

Let
(C,o)
denote our
T_{pq}
germ. Then, from the equations, we can see that the multiplicity
|m|=|m(C,o)|
is
3
if
p=3
and
4
if
p\geq 4
; we will handle these cases separately. Note again by Theorem
5.1.5
that we need only verify the desired weight inequality for lattice points within the conductor rectangle.

Suppose first that
p>4
. Then, since
T_{pq}
germs are of tame CM type (cf. Proposition
4.4.1
), Conditions (O1a) and (O1b) of Proposition
4.4.2
tell us that
(C,o)
decomposes as a union of branches with multiplicities at most 2, and we can see from the equation (choosing
\lambda=1
) that the tangent cone at the origin is cut out by
x^{2}y^{2}
, the underlying set of which is the union of the two coordinate axes. By considering the multiplicities of the branches of the tangent cone, we can see that, if we let
(C^{\prime},o)
be the subcurve which is the union of all branches with reduced tangent cone the
x
-axis and
(C^{\prime\prime},o)
the corresponding subcurve for the
y
-axis,
C=C^{\prime}\cup C^{\prime\prime}
and each subcurve has multiplicity 2 at the origin
o
. Thus, using our eventual Proposition
8.1.1
and the description of the reduced tangent cone, we can see that
(C^{\prime},o)
(respectively,
(C^{\prime\prime},o)
) is a singularity of type
A_{n^{\prime}}
for some
n^{\prime}\geq 2
(resp.,
A_{n^{\prime\prime}}
for some
n^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
).

In particular, using the explicit equations for
A
-type singularities, we can see that the vector
v
of orders of vanishing of some power series
s
equivalent modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{2}
to the coordinate function
y
along the branches of
(C^{\prime},o)
is given by either
(n^{\prime}+1)
(if
n^{\prime}
is even) or
((n^{\prime}+1)/2,(n^{\prime}+1)/2)
(if
n^{\prime}
is odd); we have a similar vector
w
and power series
t
equivalent modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{2}
to
x
for
C^{\prime\prime}
. Now, for any
\ell=(\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime})\leq(v,w)
in the lattice of
(C,o)
, we have
\mathscr{F}^{C}(\ell)=\mathscr{F}^{C^{\prime}}(\ell^{\prime})\cap\mathscr{F}^{C^{\prime\prime}}(\ell^{\prime\prime})
, hence
\mathfrak{h}^{C}(\ell)=\mathfrak{h}^{C^{\prime}}(\ell^{\prime})+\mathfrak{h}^{C^{\prime\prime}}(\ell^{\prime\prime})-H
, where
H:=\dim(\mathscr{O}/(\mathscr{F}^{C^{\prime}}(\ell^{\prime})+\mathscr{F}^{C^{\prime\prime}}(\ell^{\prime\prime})))
. However,
s\in\mathscr{F}^{C^{\prime}}(v)\subseteq\mathscr{F}^{C^{\prime}}(\ell^{\prime})
and, similarly,
t\in\mathscr{F}^{C^{\prime\prime}}(\ell^{\prime\prime})
; thus,
\mathscr{F}^{C^{\prime}}(\ell^{\prime})+\mathscr{F}^{C^{\prime\prime}}(\ell^{\prime\prime})\supseteq\mathfrak{m}
and so
H\leq 1
. Hence,
w_{0}^{C}(\ell)\geq w_{0}^{C^{\prime}}(\ell^{\prime})+w_{0}^{C^{\prime\prime}}(\ell^{\prime\prime})-2
, while
w_{0}^{C^{\prime}}(\ell^{\prime})\geq 0
and
w_{0}^{C^{\prime\prime}}(\ell^{\prime\prime})\geq 0
since
(C^{\prime},o)
and
(C^{\prime\prime},o)
are
A
-germs (see Subsections
6.2
and
6.3
); it follows that
w_{0}(\ell)\geq-2
for such
\ell
.

Now, for any lattice point
0\leq\ell\leq c
, observe by (
2.2.8
) and the duality (
2.3.2
) that
2w_{0}(\ell)=w_{0}(\ell)+w_{0}(c-\ell)\geq w_{0}(\min\{\ell,c-\ell\})+w_{0}(\max\{\ell,c-\ell\})=w_{0}(\min\{\ell,c-\ell\})+w_{0}(c-\max\{\ell,c-\ell\})=2w_{0}(\min\{\ell,c-\ell\})
. Therefore, it suffices to verify that
c\leq 2(v,w)+e
in these cases, so that
\min\{\ell,c-\ell\}\leq(v,w)
; this can be observed directly by considering the possible combinations of parities of
n^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime\prime}
separately and using standard formulae for the conductor of a plane curve singularity in terms of the conductors and intersection multiplicities of its components.

We have already verified the case
4=p=q
in Subsection
6.7
. The case
4=p<q
is similar to our work above, although now the reduced tangent cone is the union of three lines rather than two and we decompose our curve into an
A_{1}
singularity and an
A_{\geq 2}
singularity; accordingly, we obtain only
H\leq 2
in general and must use some reasoning about the behavior of
w_{0}^{A_{1}}
to make up the difference. Among the
p=3
cases, we have already addressed the situations where
q=6
or
q
is odd in Subsections
6.8
and
6.9
respectively. In the remaining cases, the reduced tangent cone will be a single line—nevertheless, we can make an argument along the same lines as for
4=p<q
, where
H\leq 2
in general and we use the particular behavior of the weight function
w_{0}^{A_{0}}
along the smooth branch to make up the difference.
∎

Remark 6.11.2
.

In fact, one can prove (see, e.g.,
[
50
]
) that, along
\delta
-constant deformations such as
C_{t,s}:=\{(x^{2a+1}+tx^{2a}+y^{2})(x^{2}+sy^{2b}+y^{2b+1})=0\}
(for
t,s\in(\mathbb{C},0)
), the lattice homologies
\mathbb{H}_{*}(C_{t,s},o)
stay stable and hence
\min w_{0}^{C_{t,s}}
is constant too. Thus we can also prove the preceding result by computing some of the cases explicitly, as in Subsection
6.10
, and using deformations to establish the bound for the rest.

7.
Curves of finite Cohen-Macaulay type

7.1.

In this section, we characterize finite-Cohen-Macaulay-type curve germs in terms of the weight function
w_{0}
, arriving at a description which depends only on the usual analytic lattice homology. Throughout, we will let
(C,o)
be a reduced complex-analytic curve germ and use the notations of Section
2
.

Proposition 7.1.1

(First
w_{0}
-characterization of finite CM type)
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

(C,o)
is of finite Cohen-Macaulay type.

(2)

w_{0}(m)\geq-1
and
w_{0}(2m)\geq 0
.

(3)

w_{0}(\ell)\geq-1
for
\ell\in\{m,2m\}
.

Proof.

The proof is based on Proposition
4.2.4
: the condition (1) is equivalent to the conjunction of the inequalities (a)
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\leq 3
and (b)
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O})/(\mathfrak{m}^{2}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O})\leq 1
. By (
2.2.6
),
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}=|m|=2-w_{0}(m)
, and hence (a) is equivalent to
w_{0}(m)\geq-1
. On the other hand, by (
2.2.7
), (b) is equivalent to
w_{0}(2m)\geq 0
and so (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2) follows. To see (2)
\Leftrightarrow
(3), we note that
|2m|
is even; therefore, by (
2.2.4
),
w_{0}(2m)\in 2\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

The following formulation is more concise and gives a condition depending only on
\mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o)
:

Theorem 7.1.2

(Second
w_{0}
-characterization of finite CM type)
.

(C,o)
is of finite Cohen-Macaulay type if and only if
\min w_{0}^{C}\geq-1
.

Proof.

By the implication (3)
\Rightarrow
(1) of Proposition
7.1.1
, the inequality
\min w_{0}\geq-1
implies that
(C,o)
is of finite CM type. For the other direction, we provide three proofs, each from a different perspective; the first is based on the dominance relation, via our prior explicit computations and Proposition
4.2.3
, while the second and third are based on Proposition
4.2.4
via algebraic and semigroup-theoretic arguments respectively.

First proof.
If
(C,o)
is of finite Cohen-Macaulay type, then by Proposition
4.2.3
it birationally dominates an
ADE
germ. Since for any
ADE
germ one has
\min w_{0}\geq-1
(see Section
6
), Lemma
3.2.2
(g) gives
\min w_{0}^{C}\geq-1
as well.

Second proof.
As mentioned, we use Proposition
4.2.4
; the concept is that Condition (a) bounds the
{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}
-values of lattice points in
R(0,m)
, while Condition (b) provides a bound (in terms of
\mathfrak{h}(\ell)
) for the change in
{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}
from
\min\{\ell,m\}
to
\ell
for each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
. To this end, suppose that
(C,o)
is of finite Cohen-Macaulay type and fix
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
; since
w_{0}(0)=0
, we can take
|\ell|>0
. Then
w_{0}(\ell)=\mathfrak{h}(\ell)-{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\ell)=\mathfrak{h}(\ell)-({\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\ell)-{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\min\{\ell,m\}))-{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\min\{\ell,m\})
, so it is enough to show
\mathfrak{h}(\ell)-({\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\ell)-{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\min\{\ell,m\}))\geq 1
and
{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\min\{\ell,m\})\leq 2
.

For the latter inequality, we note that
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\min\{\ell,m\})={\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
and so
{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\min\{\ell,m\})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/({\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}))\leq\dim_{\mathbb{C}}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/(\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}))=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}-\dim_{\mathbb{C}}((\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O})/\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}-\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{O}/\mathfrak{m}=|m|-1
; thus Proposition
4.2.4
(a) gives the desired result.

For the former, we observe that
{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\ell)-{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\min\{\ell,m\})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(({\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O})/({\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathscr{O}))
; by the usual module isomorphism theorems, this is equal to
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/(\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cap\mathscr{O})}{(\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cap({\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathscr{O}))/(\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cap\mathscr{O})}
. By considering the natural surjection
\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{m}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O})
, we find that this can be written as
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\mathfrak{m}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O})}{\mathfrak{m}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O})\cap(({\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathscr{O}))/\mathscr{O})}
, and similar reasoning lets us rewrite Condition (b) of Proposition
4.2.4
as
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\mathfrak{m}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O})}{\mathfrak{m}^{2}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O})}\leq 1
. Then, by Nakayama’s Lemma,
\mathfrak{m}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O})\cong\mathscr{O}/J
for some ideal
J\subseteq\mathscr{O}
; hence
\mathfrak{m}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O})\cap(({\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathscr{O})/\mathscr{O})
is identified with with
I(\mathscr{O}/J)
for some ideal
I\supseteq J
of
\mathscr{O}
, giving
\frac{\mathfrak{m}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O})}{\mathfrak{m}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O})\cap(({\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathscr{O})/\mathscr{O})}\cong\frac{\mathscr{O}/J}{I(\mathscr{O}/J)}\cong\mathscr{O}/I
. Explicitly, if we let
M:=\frac{{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O}}{({\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathscr{O})/\mathscr{O}}
, so that
\mathfrak{m}M=\frac{\mathfrak{m}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O})}{\mathfrak{m}({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\mathscr{O})\cap(({\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathscr{O})/\mathscr{O})}
, we can write
I=\ann_{\mathscr{O}}\mathfrak{m}M=\ann_{\mathscr{O}}\frac{{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}}{{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)+\mathscr{O}}
; note in particular that
\mathscr{F}(\ell)={\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)\cap\mathscr{O}
is contained in
I
and indeed in
\ann_{\mathscr{O}}M\subseteq I
.

Hence our desired inequality is
1\leq\mathfrak{h}(\ell)-({\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\ell)-{\overline{\mathfrak{h}}}(\min\{\ell,m\}))=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{O}/\mathscr{F}(\ell)-\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{O}/I=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}I/\mathscr{F}(\ell)
, so it suffices to prove that one of the containments
I\supseteq\ann_{\mathscr{O}}M\supseteq\mathscr{F}(\ell)
is strict. Since
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)
is Artinian, some power of
\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
must be contained in
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)
; it follows that some power of
\mathfrak{m}
annihilates
M
as well. Let
E\in\mathbb{N}
be minimal such that
\mathfrak{m}^{E}\subseteq\ann_{\mathscr{O}}M
. Since
|\ell|>0
, we can see that
E>0
as well; otherwise, we would have
\mathscr{F}(\ell)=(1)
in
\mathscr{O}
and thus
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\ell)=(1)
in
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
. Thus
\mathfrak{m}^{E}\subseteq\ann_{\mathscr{O}}M
implies
\mathfrak{m}^{E-1}\subseteq\ann_{\mathscr{O}}\mathfrak{m}M
, and so
\ann_{\mathscr{O}}M\neq\ann_{\mathscr{O}}\mathfrak{m}M
by the minimality of
E
.

Third proof.
We prove that Condition (3) of Proposition
7.1.1
(a property formulated merely for
m
and
2m
) implies
\min w_{0}\geq-1
via the general connection (
2.2.4
) between
w_{0}
and the semigroup
\mathscr{S}
of
(C,o)
. For brevity, we abbreviate the reference (
2.2.4
) by
({\dagger})
.

Suppose
(C,o)
is of finite CM type, so that the aforementioned condition holds. Then, by (
2.2.6
),
|m|\leq 3
. If
|m|\leq 2
,
(C,o)
is an
A_{n}
germ for some
n\geq 0
and hence satisfies
\min w_{0}^{C}=0
by Proposition
8.1.1
; thus we need only verify the case with
|m|=3
(and so
r\leq 3
).

Claim 1:

If, for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
,
w_{0}(\ell+e^{i})-w_{0}(\ell)=1
, then
w_{0}(s+\ell-\ell^{\prime}+e^{i})-w_{0}(s+\ell-\ell^{\prime})=1
too for any
s\in\mathscr{S}
and
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
with
\ell_{i}=0
. Indeed, by
({\dagger})
, the first identity is equivalent to
{\overline{\Delta}}_{i}(\ell)\neq\emptyset
, but then
{\overline{\Delta}}_{i}(s+\ell-\ell^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
too, since this contains
s+{\overline{\Delta}}_{i}(\ell)
.

Claim 2:

w_{0}(\ell)\geq-1
for any lattice point
m\leq\ell\leq 2m
. Indeed, along any ‘path’
m
,
m+e^{i}
,
m+e^{i}+e^{j}
,
2m
the value of
w_{0}
either increases by 1 or decreases by 1 at each step (cf.
({\dagger})
). At the first step, it increases from
-1
to
0
(since
m\in\mathscr{S}
), and there must exist one more step when it increases since
w_{0}(2m)\geq-1
by hypothesis. The claim follows.

Now, if we shift (any path of) the rectangle
R(m,2m)
by
km\in\mathscr{S}
(for
k\geq 0
), the two claims imply
w_{0}(\ell)\geq-1
for any lattice point
km\leq\ell\leq(k+1)m
. This fact already ends the proof for
r=1
; assume next that
r=2
and so, up to reordering,
m=(2,1)
. Consider
\ell=(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
with
2\ell_{2}>\ell_{1}
. Then there is a sequence of lattice points with increasing second coordinate from
(\ell_{1},\lceil\ell_{1}/2\rceil)
, which lies between successive multiples of
m
, to
\ell
. Since each
km\in\mathscr{S}
,
w_{0}
is increasing along this path by
({\dagger})
, hence
w_{0}(\ell)\geq-1
. Similarly, consider
\ell=(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
with
2\ell_{2}<\ell_{1}
.
Then there is a sequence of lattice points with increasing first coordinate from
(2\ell_{2},\ell_{2})
to
\ell
. By
({\dagger})
,
w_{0}
increases at all odd steps along this path, so
w_{0}(\ell)\geq-1
.

Finally, assume that
r=3
, hence
m=(1,1,1)
. We prove
w_{0}(\ell)\geq-1
by decreasing induction on
d(\ell):=\max_{i}\{\ell_{i}\}-\min_{i}\{\ell_{i}\}
. If
d(\ell)\leq 1
, then
km\leq\ell\leq(k+1)m
for some
k\geq 0
, hence
w_{0}(\ell)\geq-1
. Fix
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
with
d(\ell)>1
and assume without loss of generality that
\ell_{1}\leq\ell_{2}\leq\ell_{3}
. If
\ell_{3}>\ell_{2}
, then
w_{0}(\ell)>w_{0}(\ell-e^{3})\geq 1
by
(\ell_{3}-1)m\in{\overline{\Delta}}_{3}(\ell-e^{3})
and induction. If
\ell_{2}=\ell_{3}
, then by (the proof of) Claim 2 at least one of the inequalities
w_{0}(m+e^{1}+e^{i})>w_{0}(m+e^{1})
for
i\in\{2,3\}
holds. Hence, by Claim 1, one of the inequalities
w_{0}(\ell)>w_{0}(\ell-e^{i})
for
i\in\{2,3\}
holds as well.
∎

Corollary 7.1.3
.

Suppose that
(C,o)
is Gorenstein. Then
(C,o)
is an
ADE
germ if and only if
\min w_{0}^{C}\geq-1
. In particular, a reduced plane curve singularity is
ADE
if and only if
\min w_{0}\geq-1
.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.1.2
,
\min w_{0}\geq-1
if and only if
(C,o)
is of finite CM type. On the other hand, by, e.g.,
[
54
, Chapter 8]
, a Gorenstein singularity of finite CM type is
ADE
. (The statement also follows from a combination of Theorem
7.1.2
, Proposition
4.2.3
, and the list from Example
3.2.3
(3).)
∎

Since for plane curve singularities
w_{0}
depends only on the embedded topological type of
(C,o)\subset(\mathbb{C}^{2},o)
, the second statement of the above corollary is a new embedded topological characterization of simple germs.

8.
Curves of finite Cohen-Macaulay type. Finer characterizations

Recall that, by Proposition
4.2.3
, a reduced curve singularity is of finite CM type if and only if it birationally dominates an
ADE
germ. In this section, we obtain conditions distinguishing those germs which birationally dominate an
A
germ, as well as those dominating germs of type
AD
. Our goal is to separate these families of finite-CM-type singularities using the language of lattice homology and its associated constructions. These distinctions provide a finer classification of finite-CM-type germs, and the corresponding subclasses will be used in our characterization of tame-CM-type germs as well. As usual, we let
(C,o)
be a reduced complex-analytic curve germ and adopt the conventions of Section
2
; we will also use the machinery of Subsection
5.3
.

8.1.
Birational dominance and
A_{n}
germs

Proposition 8.1.1
.

The following are equivalent:

(1)

(C,o)
has multiplicity at most 2 at
o
.

(2)

\min w_{0}^{C}=0
.

(3)

(C,o)
birationally dominates a simple germ of type
A_{n}
(for some
n\geq 0
).

(4)

(C,o)
is a simple germ of type
A_{n}
(for some
n\geq 0
).

Proof.

(4)
\Rightarrow
(3) is clear. (3)
\Rightarrow
(2) follows from Lemma
3.2.2
(g) and
\min w_{0}^{A_{n}}\geq 0
(see Subsections
6.2
and
6.3
). For (2)
\Rightarrow
(1), we can use (
2.2.6
) with
\ell=m
. Finally, to show (1)
\Rightarrow
(4), we use the Abhyankar inequality (
[
1
]
) to see that the embedding dimension
\edim(C,o)\leq|m|\leq 2
. Hence
(C,o)
is a plane curve with multiplicity
\leq 2
, so by the splitting theorem (e.g.,
[
5
, Section 11.1]
) it is of type
A_{n}
.
∎

8.2.
Birational dominance and
D_{n}
germs

Proposition 8.2.1
.

Suppose that
(C,o)
does not satisfy the equivalent conditions of Proposition
8.1.1
. Then
(C,o)
birationally dominates a simple singularity of type
D_{n}
(for some
n\geq 4
) if and only if
\min w_{0}^{C}=-1
and
(C,o)
has minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight 0.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
) Assume that
(C,o)
birationally dominates some
D_{n}
for
n\geq 4
. Then, by Lemma
3.2.2
(g),
\min w^{C}_{0}\geq\min w_{0}^{D_{n}}=-1
(see Subsections
6.4
and
6.5
); our hypothesis that
(C,o)
does not satisfy the conditions of Proposition
8.1.1
and (
2.2.6
) then give us
\min w^{C}_{0}=-1
,
w_{0}^{C}(m)=-1
, and
|m|=3
. It remains to prove the existence of a minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight 0. Since the number of components of
D_{n}
is 2 or 3, Lemma
3.2.2
(b) gives
r\in\{2,3\}
.

Suppose first that
r=2
, so that
n
is odd, and order the branches so that
m=(2,1)
(this then holds for the dominated
D_{n}
germ as well by Lemma
3.2.2
(d)). Since
m\in\mathscr{S}
, (
2.2.4
) gives us
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1})=w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{2})=0
and thus
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1}+e^{2})=\pm 1
. However, since
w_{0}^{D_{n}}(m+e^{1}+e^{2})=w_{0}^{D_{n}}((3,2))=1
by Subsection
6.4
, Lemma
3.2.2
(g) gives
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1}+e^{2})=1
and so we can see that
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)=(E^{1}_{-3,4})_{0}=(E^{1}_{-m,4})_{0}\neq 0
.

Assume next that
r=3
, so that
n
is even and
m=(1,1,1)
. As before,
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i})=0
for any
1\leq i\leq r
and
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i}+e^{j})=\pm 1
for any
1\leq i<j\leq r
. Lemma
3.2.2
(g) and Subsection
6.5
then imply that
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i}+e^{j})=1
for at least two choices of
i
and
j
; we can thus see again that
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)=(E^{1}_{-3,4})_{0}=(E^{1}_{-m,4})_{0}\neq 0
.

(
\Leftarrow
) We now suppose that
\min w_{0}^{C}=-1
and
(C,o)
has a minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight 0; by (
2.2.6
) and our hypothesis on
(C,o)
, it follows that
|m|=3
and thus
r\leq 3
as well. By, e.g., Lemma
5.3.1
, we can see that the hypothesis
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)\neq 0
gives
r>1
, so
r\in\{2,3\}
; we handle these cases separately, following the strategy of
[
54
, p. 73]
.

Suppose first that
r=2
. Then
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cong\mathbb{C}\{t_{1}\}\times\mathbb{C}\{t_{2}\}
. By reordering the branches, we can also assume that
m=(2,1)
and, correspondingly,
\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}=(({t_{1}}^{2},0),(0,t_{2}))
. By Lemmas
5.3.1
and
5.3.2
,
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)\neq 0
gives
w_{0}^{C}(m)=-1
,
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1})=w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{2})=0
, and
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1}+e^{2})=1
. Since
m+e^{1}=(3,1)
and
m+e^{1}+e^{2}=(3,2)
,
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(m+e^{1})=(({t_{1}}^{3},0),(0,t_{2}))
and
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(m+e^{1}+e^{2})=(({t_{1}}^{3},0),(0,{t_{2}}^{2}))
. By Lemma
2.2.5
, we thus obtain an element of
\mathscr{O}
which is equivalent to
(0,t_{2})
modulo
(({t_{1}}^{3},0),(0,{t_{2}}^{2}))
. That is, if we view
\mathscr{O}
as a subring of
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cong\mathbb{C}\{t_{1}\}\times\mathbb{C}\{t_{2}\}
, we can see that
\left(\sum_{i=3}^{\infty}a_{i}{t_{1}}^{i},t_{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}b_{i}{t_{2}}^{i}\right)\in\mathscr{O}
for some coefficients
a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
making the resulting power series converge.

Likewise, applying Lemma
2.2.5
to
m+e^{2}
and
m+e^{1}+e^{2}
reveals that
\mathscr{O}
contains an element which is a scalar multiple of
({t_{1}}^{2},0)
modulo
(({t_{1}}^{3},0),(0,{t_{2}}^{2}))
; that is, we have
\left({t_{1}}^{2}+\sum_{i=3}^{\infty}c_{i}{t_{1}}^{i},\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}d_{i}{t_{2}}^{i}\right)\in\mathscr{O}
for some coefficients
c_{i},d_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
making the resulting power series convergent.

By taking an appropriate change of the second coordinate for
\mathbb{C}\{t_{1}\}\times\mathbb{C}\{t_{2}\}
, we can suppose without loss of generality that
b_{i}=0
for all
i\geq 2
, so that our first element becomes
\left(\sum_{i=3}^{\infty}a_{i}{t_{1}}^{i},t_{2}\right)
. Then, since
\mathscr{O}
contains any convergent power series in this element by standard properties of convergent power series rings, we can subtract from our second element appropriately to take
d_{i}=0
for all
i\geq 2
. Using the fact that such rings are Henselian, we can then change our first coordinate to guarantee
c_{i}=0
for all
i\geq 3
as well, so that our second element becomes simply
({t_{1}}^{2},0)
. Since the birationality of the normalization map implies that the conductor ideal has finite
\mathbb{C}
-codimension in
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
and hence that
(g(t_{1}),0)\in\mathscr{O}
for all
g(t_{1})\in\mathbb{C}\{t_{1}\}
with sufficiently high order of vanishing, we can then take an appropriate
\mathbb{C}
-algebraic combination of
({t_{1}}^{2},0)
,
\left(\sum_{i=3}^{\infty}a_{i}{t_{1}}^{i},t_{2}\right)
, and a chosen high-order element to suppose that
a_{i}\neq 0
for exactly one value of
i
, which is odd—that is, for some odd
n\geq 5
, we have
(a_{n-2}{t_{1}}^{n-2},t_{2})\in\mathscr{O}
. Taking a
\mathbb{C}
-linear change of our first coordinate then allows us to suppose that
({t_{1}}^{n-2},t_{2})\in\mathscr{O}
while still preserving the containment
({t_{1}}^{2},0)\in\mathscr{O}
. Therefore, as in
[
54
]
, we have
\mathbb{C}\{x,y\}/(y(x^{2}-y^{n-2}))\cong\mathbb{C}\{({t_{1}}^{n-2},t_{2}),({t_{1}}^{2},0)\}\subseteq\mathscr{O}
as a subring, and moreover since
n
is odd we can see that the composed map from the normalization of
C
to the corresponding curve germ is of degree 1 over each branch. Hence
C
birationally dominates the simple singularity of the corresponding type
D_{n}
, as desired.

Now suppose that
r=3
, so that
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cong\mathbb{C}\{t_{1}\}\times\mathbb{C}\{t_{2}\}\times\mathbb{C}\{t_{3}\}
. In this case, we necessarily have
m=(1,1,1)
and hence
\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}=\{(t_{1},0,0),(0,t_{2},0),(0,0,t_{3})\}
. Since we have a minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight 0, Lemmas
5.3.1
and
5.3.2
tell us that, up to a possible reordering of the branches,
w_{0}^{C}(m)=-1
,
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1})=w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{2})=0
, and
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1}+e^{2})=1
. By Lemma
2.2.5
, we can then see that
\mathscr{O}
contains two elements which are scalar multiples of
(0,t_{2},0)
and
(t_{1},0,0)
respectively modulo
(({t_{1}}^{2},0,0),(0,{t_{2}}^{2},0),(0,0,t_{3}))
; we can write these in turn as
\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}{t_{1}}^{i},t_{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\beta_{i}{t_{2}}^{i},\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{i}{t_{3}}^{i}\right)
and
\left(t_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\delta_{i}{t_{1}}^{i},\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\varepsilon_{i}{t_{2}}^{i},\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{i}{t_{3}}^{i}\right)
for some
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\zeta_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
. Moreover, since
\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}=\{(t_{1},0,0),(0,t_{2},0),(0,0,t_{3})\}
, we can see by considering the orders of vanishing of the generators of
\mathfrak{m}
along each branch that
\mathscr{O}
must contain an element with all such orders equal to 1; with an appropriate scaling of our coordinates and other chosen elements, we can take this to have the form
\left(t_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\eta_{i}{t_{1}}^{i},{t_{2}}+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\theta_{i}{t_{2}}^{i},t_{3}+\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\iota_{i}{t_{3}}^{i}\right)
for
\eta_{i},\theta_{i},\iota_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.

If
\gamma_{1}\neq 0
, we can proceed as follows. By subtracting appropriate convergent power series of order at least 2 in our third and second elements, we can take
\alpha_{i}=\gamma_{i}=0
for all
i\geq 2
, and then by a change of the second coordinate we can take
\beta_{i}=0
for all
i\geq 2
as well. Hence our first element becomes
(0,t_{2},\gamma_{1}t_{3})
. We can then take a change of the first coordinate and subtract the appropriate convergent power series in our first element from our third element to put it in the form
(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}+p(t_{3}))
for some
p(t_{3})\in\mathbb{C}\{t_{3}\}
of order at least 2. Then, if we let
x:=(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}+p(t_{3}))
be our third element and
y:=(0,t_{2},\gamma_{1}t_{3})
, the subring of
\mathscr{O}
consisting of convergent power series in
x
and
y
is given by
\mathbb{C}\{x,y\}/(y(x-y)(x-\frac{y}{\gamma_{1}}-p(\frac{y}{\gamma_{1}})))
, as in
[
54
]
. This corresponds to a curve germ of multiplicity 3 with either 2 or 3 tangent directions; this germ is thus a simple singularity of type
D_{n}
for some
n\geq 4
by
[
54
, p. 63]
, and
(C,o)
birationally dominates it by the containment of power series rings and the observation that the map from the normalization is again of degree 1 over each branch.

If
\zeta_{1}\neq 0
, we can repeat the same reasoning with our second element in place of the first to get elements
x:=(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}+p(t_{3}))
(with
p(t_{3})\in\mathbb{C}\{t_{3}\}
again of order at least 2) and
y:=(t_{1},0,\zeta_{1}t_{3})
generating a subring corresponding to a simple singularity of type
D_{n}
for some
n\geq 4
. On the other hand, if
\gamma_{1}=\zeta_{1}=0
, we can add our first and second elements, subtract a convergent power series in the third, and then take changes of our first and second coordinates to obtain
(t_{1},t_{2},0)\in\mathscr{O}
; taking a change of the third coordinate and subtracting a convergent power series in this new element then lets us write our third element in the form
(t_{1}+p(t_{1}),t_{2},t_{3})
with
p(t_{1})\in\mathbb{C}\{t_{1}\}
of order at least two as before, and so the reasoning of
[
54
]
again gives the desired result.
∎

8.3.
Birational dominance and
E_{6},E_{7},E_{8}
germs

Theorem
7.1.2
and Propositions
8.1.1
and
8.2.1
together imply the following:

Proposition 8.3.1
.

Suppose
(C,o)
does not birationally dominate a simple singularity of type
A_{n}
(for any
n\geq 0
) or
D_{n}
(for any
n\geq 4
). Then
(C,o)
birationally dominates a simple singularity of type
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
, or
E_{8}
if and only if
\min w_{0}^{C}=-1
.

This proposition can also be proved directly (without using Theorem
7.1.2
) through a similar strategy as in the proof of Proposition
8.2.1
, again following
[
54
, p. 72]
by way of Lemma
2.2.5
(applied to well-chosen lattice points).

9.
Curves of tame Cohen-Macaulay type. Reformulation by weights

9.1.
Setup

In this section, we rewrite the so-called overring conditions of Proposition
4.4.2
in terms of the weight function
w_{0}
. As the name suggests, these conditions are formulated in terms of several subrings of
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
containing
\mathscr{O}
; for our later convenience, we preface the discussion with a minor lemma dealing with such objects:

Lemma 9.1.1
.

Let
(\mathscr{O},\mathfrak{m})
be any reduced local ring and denote by
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
its integral closure in its total fraction ring. Let
I
be an ideal of
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
and consider the
\mathscr{O}
-subalgebra
\tilde{\mathscr{O}}:=I+\mathscr{O}
of
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
generated by
I
and
\mathscr{O}
. Then
\len_{\mathscr{O}}\frac{I+\mathscr{O}}{\mathfrak{m}I+\mathscr{O}}=-1+\len_{\mathscr{O}}\tilde{\mathscr{O}}/\mathfrak{m}\tilde{\mathscr{O}}
.

Proof.

First use
\frac{\tilde{\mathscr{O}}}{\mathfrak{m}\tilde{\mathscr{O}}}=\frac{I+\mathscr{O}}{\mathfrak{m}I+\mathfrak{m}}
and the short exact sequence
0\to\frac{\mathfrak{m}I+\mathscr{O}}{\mathfrak{m}I+\mathfrak{m}}\to\frac{I+\mathscr{O}}{\mathfrak{m}I+\mathfrak{m}}\to\frac{I+\mathscr{O}}{\mathfrak{m}I+\mathscr{O}}\to 0
, then note that, by some standard isomorphisms and the containment
\mathfrak{m}I\cap\mathscr{O}\subseteq\mathfrak{m}
,
\frac{\mathfrak{m}I+\mathscr{O}}{\mathfrak{m}I+\mathfrak{m}}\cong\frac{\mathscr{O}}{\mathfrak{m}}
, which has length 1.
∎

(In the cases we are interested in, length over
\mathscr{O}
coincides with dimension as a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space, which is the formulation we use in the majority of this note.)

9.2.
Results

Let
(C,o)
be a reduced complex-analytic curve germ; we use the conventions of Section
2
, and will also make free use of the various notations introduced in Proposition
4.4.2
.

Recall that, by (
2.2.6
), Condition (O1a)—that is,
|m|\leq 4
—is already equivalent to
\min w_{0}^{C}\geq-2
. We proceed to the reformulation of the other conditions:

Lemma 9.2.1
.

Assume that
(C,o)
satisfies (O1a). Then
(C,o)
satisfies (O1b) if and only if
w_{0}^{C_{i}}(m_{i})\geq 0
for all
1\leq i\leq r
.

Proof.

By (O1a),
\sum_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}\leq 4
. Then, by considering all partitions of the integers 1, 2, 3, and 4 and observing that
\{[4],[1,3],[3]\}
is exactly the collection of such partitions which have a part of size greater than 2, we see that (O2b) holds if and only if
m_{i}\leq 2
for any
i
; the result follows by (
2.2.6
).
∎

Lemma 9.2.2
.

(C,o)
satisfies (O2a) if and only if
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)\geq|m|-r-2
.

Proof.

Condition (O2a) holds exactly when
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}}{\mathfrak{m}(t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O})}\leq 3
, which is, by Lemma
9.1.1
, equivalent to
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}}{t\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}}\leq 2
. Since
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(m)=\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
and
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}(e)=t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
, by (
2.1.4
) this in turn is equivalent to
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)-w_{0}^{C}(e)\geq|m|-4
. The result now follows since
w_{0}^{C}(e)=2-r
by (
2.2.6
).
∎

Remark 9.2.3
.

For our next result, it will be useful to note that a stronger version of the inequality of Lemma
9.2.2
holds automatically for the
A_{n}
germs: If
|m|\leq 2
(cf. Proposition
8.1.1
), then
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)\geq|m|-r
. This can be seen directly from the computations in Subsections
6.2
and
6.3
.

Lemma 9.2.4
.

Suppose
(C,o)
satisfies (O1a), (O1b), and (O2a). Then
(C,o)
satisfies (O2b) if and only if the following holds for each reduced subcurve germ
(\hat{C},o):=(C_{J},o)
with
|J|=r-1
: If we let
\hat{m}:=m(\hat{C},o)\in\mathbb{N}^{r-1}
be the multiplicity vector of
(\hat{C},o)
,
\hat{e}\in\mathbb{N}^{r-1}
be the all-ones vector, and
\hat{r}:=r-1
, then
w_{0}^{\hat{C}}(\hat{m}+\hat{e})\geq|\hat{m}|-\hat{r}
.

Moreover, this latter condition will always hold unless
|m|=4
and the branch excluded from
(\hat{C},o)
has multiplicity 1, and hence it is equivalent to require it only in these circumstances.

Proof.

If
r=1
, we find that both sides of our claimed equivalence are trivially satisfied, since in this case there are no such subcurve germs and
\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}={\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cong\mathbb{C}\{t_{1}\}
, which gives
\lambda(\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime})=\lambda({\overline{\mathscr{O}}})=m=(m_{1})\neq(1,3)
.

Therefore, we can suppose henceforth that
r\geq 2
. Fix an index
1\leq i\leq r
to show that
\vec{\lambda}(\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime})\neq(1,3)
if and only if our stated weight condition holds for
(\hat{C},o)=(\hat{C}_{i},o):=\bigcup_{j\neq i}(C_{i},o)
. By considering the idempotent
\epsilon_{i}\in\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}
, we obtain a product decomposition
\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}\cong\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}\epsilon_{i}\times\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})
. In particular, this allows us to decompose
\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}/\mathfrak{m}\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}
as the product of
\frac{\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}\epsilon_{i}}{\mathfrak{m}\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}\epsilon_{i}}
and
\frac{\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})}{\mathfrak{m}\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})}
. Observe that
\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}
contains the ideal
(\epsilon_{i})=\mathbb{C}\{t_{i}\}\epsilon_{i}
of
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
generated by the idempotent
\epsilon_{i}
, so that
\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}\epsilon_{i}={\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}\cong\mathbb{C}\{t_{i}\}
and, indeed, the ring map
\mathscr{O}\to\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}\epsilon_{i}
factors as
\mathscr{O}\hookrightarrow{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}\cong\mathbb{C}\{t_{i}\}
. Hence
\frac{\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}\epsilon_{i}}{\mathfrak{m}\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}\epsilon_{i}}\cong\mathbb{C}\{t_{i}\}/({t_{i}}^{m_{i}})
is an Artinian local
\mathscr{O}
-algebra with length equal to the multiplicity
m_{i}
of
(C_{i},o)
.

Moreover, since we also obtain the equality
\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathscr{O}+t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathbb{C}\epsilon_{i}=\mathscr{O}+t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}
, we have an identification
\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})\cong\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}/(\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}\epsilon_{i})\cong(\mathscr{O}+t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i})/({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i})
; observe that this is a local ring with maximal ideal
(t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i})/({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i})
. Thus the quotient
\frac{\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})}{\mathfrak{m}\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})}
is an Artinian local
\mathscr{O}
-algebra as well; its length and
m_{i}
, in non-decreasing order, are hence the two entries of the vector
\lambda(\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime})
.

By Lemma
9.2.1
and (
2.2.6
), Condition (O1b) guarantees that
m_{i}\leq 2
. If
m_{i}=2
, then we can see immediately that
\lambda(\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime})\neq(1,3)
and so Condition (O2b) is satisfied, while our weight condition holds since
(\hat{C},o)
has multiplicity
|m|-m_{i}=|m|-2\leq 4-2=2
by Condition (O1a) and hence we can apply the bound of Remark
9.2.3
to it. (Indeed, we can see in this way that the weight condition also holds automatically if
|m|<4
, which verifies our second claim.) Thus we only have to deal with the case
m_{i}=1
, in which the condition
\lambda(\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime})\neq(1,3)
becomes equivalent to the assertion
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})}{\mathfrak{m}\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})}\neq 3
. Since
\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})\cong\mathscr{O}^{\prime}/({\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}\cap\mathscr{O}^{\prime})
is a quotient of
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}
and so
\frac{\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})}{\mathfrak{m}\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})}
is a quotient of
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}/\mathfrak{m}\mathscr{O}^{\prime}
, Condition (O2a) tells us that this length is bounded above by 3, so in fact we can here restate
\lambda(\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime})\neq(1,3)
as
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})}{\mathfrak{m}\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime}(1-\epsilon_{i})}\leq 2
; this inequality can, by applying an argument similar to the one used to prove Lemma
9.1.1
, be further simplified to
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\mathscr{O}+t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}}{\mathscr{O}+t\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}}\leq 1
.

Observe now that the quotient ring
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}/\epsilon_{i}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cong\prod_{j\neq i}\mathbb{C}\{t_{j}\}
is precisely the integral closure of the local convergent power series ring of
(\hat{C},o)
in its total fraction ring; in particular, we can identify the ideals of
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
containing
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}
with the ideals of this integral closure. Under this identification, we have
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\hat{m})=\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}
and
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(\hat{e})=t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}
; hence, by (
2.1.4
) and (
2.2.6
), we can see that

w_{0}^{\hat{C}}(\hat{m}+\hat{e})=w_{0}^{\hat{C}}(\hat{e})+|\hat{m}|-2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\tfrac{\mathscr{O}+t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}}{\mathscr{O}+t\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}}=|\hat{m}|+2-\hat{r}-2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\tfrac{\mathscr{O}+t{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}}{\mathscr{O}+t\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\epsilon_{i}}.

Thus we find that the condition
\lambda(\mathscr{O}_{i}^{\prime})\neq(1,3)
is in fact equivalent to the inequality
w_{0}^{\hat{C}}(\hat{m}+\hat{e})\geq|\hat{m}|-\hat{r}
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 9.2.5
.

Suppose that
|m|=3
. Then
(C,o)
satisfies (O3) if and only if
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e)\geq w_{0}^{C}(m+e)+1
.

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{O}^{\prime\prime}=t\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}
, Lemma
9.1.1
tells us that Condition (O3) is, under our hypothesis
|m|=3
, equivalent to the inequality
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{t\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}}{t\mathfrak{m}^{2}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}+\mathscr{O}}\leq 1
. Noting that
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(m+e)=t\mathfrak{m}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
and
{\overline{\mathscr{F}}}(2m+e)=t\mathfrak{m}^{2}{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
, we can use (
2.1.4
) to see that this condition holds if and only if
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e)-w_{0}^{C}(m+e)\geq|m|-2
; since
|m|-2=3-2=1
, the result follows.
∎

Using the result of Subsection
2.4
, we can collect the preceding lemmas into a single result which characterizes the overring conditions of Proposition
4.4.2
in terms of the weight function
w_{0}^{C}
of
(C,o)
itself (note in particular that we no longer need to appeal to explicit information about any subcurve or overring other than the normalization):

Proposition 9.2.6
.

Suppose that
(C,o)
is of infinite CM type. Then
(C,o)
is tame if and only if all of the following conditions hold:

(W1a)

w_{0}^{C}(m)\geq-2
.

(W1b)

w_{0}^{C}(m_{i}e^{i})\geq 0
for all
1\leq i\leq r
.

(W2a)

w_{0}^{C}(m+e)\geq|m|-r-2
.

(W2b)

w_{0}^{C}(m-m_{i}e^{i}+e-e^{i})\geq|m|-m_{i}-r+1
for all
1\leq i\leq r
.

(W3)

If
|m|=3
, then
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e)\geq w_{0}^{C}(m+e)+1
.

Proof.

Immediate from (
2.2.6
), the preceding lemmas, and the inclusion of Subsection
2.4
.
∎

As noted in Lemma
9.2.4
, Condition (W2b) is immediate if
|m|<4
and can be further simplified to pertain only to those indices
i
such that
m_{i}=1
.

Remark 9.2.7
.

If
(C,o)
is tame, then
r\geq 2
. This fact follows already from Proposition
4.4.1
, but also from (W1b) above (combined with Proposition
8.1.1
).

10.
Curves of tame Cohen-Macaulay type. Spectral reformulation

Having characterized tameness through the behavior of the weight function
w_{0}
, we now simplify further by reformulating it in homological terms—using the analytic lattice homology, where possible, and the associated spectral sequence, when extra information is needed. We can also rephrase the finite growth condition of Definition
4.3.3
in this language; together, these arguments will result in a proof of Theorem
2
.

As usual, let
(C,o)
be a reduced complex-analytic curve germ; we use the notations of Section
2
and the machinery of Subsection
5.3
.

10.1.
Tameness

Proposition 10.1.1
.

Suppose
(C,o)
is of tame CM type. Then
\min w_{0}^{C}=-2
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.4.1
,
(C,o)
dominates a
T_{pq}
germ for some
p\leq q
with
1/p+1/q\leq 1/2
; hence
\min w_{0}^{C}\geq\min w_{0}^{T_{pq}}\geq-2

by Lemma
3.2.2
(g) and Proposition
6.11.1
. On the other hand,

\min w_{0}^{C}\leq-2
by Theorem
7.1.2
since
(C,o)
is of infinite CM type.
∎

Proposition 10.1.2
.

Suppose that
\min w_{0}^{C}=-2
and
r\geq 2
. Then
(C,o)
satisfies Conditions (O2a) and (O3) of Proposition
4.4.2
if and only if
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)\neq 0
.

Moreover, these conditions always hold if
|m|=4
and
r>2
.

Proof.

By the hypothesis
\min w_{0}^{C}=-2
and (
2.2.6
),
(C,o)
satisfies (O1a)—that is,
|m|\leq 4
. On the other hand, by the same hypothesis and Proposition
8.1.1
, we find that
|m|>2
; hence we have
|m|\in\{3,4\}
. We will pursue these cases separately.

Suppose first that
|m|=4
, which gives us
w_{0}^{C}(m)=-2
by (
2.2.6
). Then (O3) is trivially satisfied, so we seek to prove the equivalence of (O2a) with the existence of a minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight
-1
. Note by Lemmas
5.3.1
and
5.3.2
that, in this case,
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)\neq 0
implies the existence of indices
1\leq i<j\leq r
such that
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i})=w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{j})=-1
and
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i}+e^{j})=0
. (
2.2.4
) then implies that
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)\geq w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i}+e^{j})-(r-2)=2-r=|m|-r-2
, and hence
(C,o)
satisfies (O2a) by Lemma
9.2.2
.

On the other hand, if we suppose that
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)=0
, we can use cellular computations as in the proof of Lemma
5.3.1
to obtain constraints on the weights of lattice points implying that Condition (O2a) fails. We work by cases; note that
r\leq|m|=4
and so
r\in\{2,3,4\}
by our hypothesis on
r
. If
r=2
, then by Lemma
9.2.2
Condition (O2a) is equivalent to
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1}+e^{2})\geq 0
, which must fail since otherwise the two 1-cubes rooted at
m
would produce a cycle in
(E_{-{\bf m},5})_{-1}=(E_{-4,5})_{-1}=\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)
. If
r\in\{3,4\}
, on the other hand,
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)=0
already contradicts our hypothesis on
\min w_{0}^{C}
. If this group is zero, there must exist a pair of 2-cubes of weight at most
-1
rooted at
m
which share a lower face, since otherwise we could again produce a relative cycle by considering a pair of 1-cubes (which have weight
-1
by (
2.2.4
) and the inclusion
m\in\mathscr{S}
) rooted at
m
not connected by any sequence of such 2-cubes. Hence, up to a reordering of coordinates, there must exist indices
1\leq i<j<k\leq r
such that
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i}+e^{j})=w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{j}+e^{k})=-2
by (
2.2.4
). Therefore
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i}+e^{j}+e^{k})\leq w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i}+e^{j})+w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{j}+e^{k})-w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{j})=-2-2-(-1)=-3
by (
2.2.8
), giving the desired contradiction; thus the failure of Condition (O2a) follows by explosion. (In particular, as noted, (O2a) and (O3) hold automatically when
|m|=4
and
r>2
.)

Now suppose that
|m|=3
, so that
w_{0}^{C}(m)=-1
by (
2.2.6
). Then, by (
2.2.4
) and
m\in\mathscr{S}
, we have
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i})=0
for all
1\leq i\leq r
, hence
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)\geq w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1})-(r-1)=1-r
. Thus Condition (O2a) is already satisfied by Lemma
9.2.2
, so we must prove that the existence of our 1-cycle is equivalent to Condition (O3). Now, by Proposition
7.1.1
and Theorem
7.1.2
, we must have
w_{0}^{C}(2m)<-1
, since otherwise we would find
\min w_{0}^{C}\geq-1
and so contradict our hypothesis
\min w_{0}^{C}=-2
; hence, this hypothesis guarantees that
w_{0}^{C}(2m)=-2
. Moreover, we can see as a consequence that in fact
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)=1-r
exactly, by the following reasoning. Since
r\leq|m|=3
and so
r\in\{2,3\}
by the hypothesis that
C
have more than one branch, we have either
m=(2,1)
(up to a reordering of coordinates) or
m=(1,1,1)
. In the former case, we have
w_{0}^{C}(2m)=w_{0}^{C}(m+e+e^{1})\geq w_{0}^{C}(m+e)-1
by (
2.2.4
), so
w_{0}^{C}(2m)=-2
implies
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)\leq-1=1-r
. In the latter case,
e=m
and so
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)=w_{0}^{C}(2m)=-2=1-r
. As before, (
2.2.4
) and the containment
2m\in\mathscr{S}
then give us
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{i})=-1
for each
1\leq i\leq r
, and so we can apply Lemmas
5.3.1
and
5.3.2
to see that the existence of a minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight
-1
implies that of a pair
1\leq i<j\leq r
of indices such that
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{i}+e^{j})=0
. If
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)\neq 0
, we thus have
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e)\geq w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{i}+e^{j})-(r-2)=2-r=(1-r)+1=w_{0}^{C}(m+e)+1
, so Condition (O3) holds by Lemma
9.2.5
. On the other hand, if
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)=0
, we can see by our usual explicit cellular computation of the relevant relative homology group that, in the
r=2
case,
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{1}+e^{2})\leq-1
must hold and so
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e)=w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{1}+e^{2})<0=(1-r)+1=w_{0}^{C}(m+e)+1
. In the
r=3
case,
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)=0
implies that, up to a reordering of the coordinates,
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{1}+e^{2})=w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{2}+e^{3})=-2
by our prior reasoning in the
|m|=4
,
r\in\{3,4\}
case; we can then conclude by (
2.2.8
) that
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e)=w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{1}+e^{2}+e^{3})\leq w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{1}+e^{2})+w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{2}+e^{3})-w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{2})=-2-2-(-1)=-3<-1=(1-r)+1=w_{0}^{C}(m+e)+1
. Hence in each case we find that Condition (O3) is not satisfied by Lemma
9.2.5
, concluding our proof of the equivalence.
∎

10.2.
Finite growth

Proposition 10.2.1
.

Suppose that
(C,o)
is of tame CM type and finite growth. Then
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)
is of maximal rank.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.4.1
,
(C,o)
birationally dominates either
T_{4,4}
or
T_{3,6}
, and Lemma
3.2.2
(b) then gives
r=4
in the former case and
r=3
in the latter; we deal with these separately.

If
(C,o)
dominates
T_{4,4}
, then we have
|m|\leq 4
by Condition (O1a) of Proposition
4.4.2
and
|m|\geq r=4
, so
|m|=r=4
and thus necessarily
m=e=(1,1,1,1)
. By (
2.2.6
),
w_{0}^{C}(m)=-2
; using (
2.2.4
), Lemma
3.2.2
(g), and the computations of Subsection
6.7
, we now find that, for each
I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}
,
w_{0}^{C}(m+\sum_{i\in I}e^{i})=|I|-2
. A direct computation, as in the proof of Lemma
5.3.1
, now gives us
\rk\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}=\rk(E^{1}_{-4,5})_{2}=\rk(E^{1}_{-m,5})_{2}=3
, as desired.

If
(C,o)
dominates
T_{3,6}
, Lemma
3.2.2
(b–d) and the fact that
|m|>2
(by tameness and, e.g., Proposition
8.1.1
) guarantee that
m=(1,1,1)
, so
w_{0}^{C}(m)=-1
by (
2.2.6
); the fact that
(C,o)
is of infinite CM type then guarantees that
w_{0}^{C}(2m)=-2
by (
2.2.4
),
m\in\mathscr{S}
, and Proposition
7.1.1
. As in the previous case, we can now use Lemma
3.2.2
(g) and the results of Subsection
6.8
to show that
(E^{1}_{-6,7})_{2}
obtains the maximal rank of 2.
∎

Proposition 10.2.2
.

Suppose that
(C,o)
is of tame CM type and
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)
is of maximal rank. Then
(C,o)
is of finite growth.

Proof.

We take an approach similar to that of the proof of Proposition
8.2.1
. Condition (O1a) of Proposition
4.4.2
and Proposition
8.1.1
give us
|m|\in\{3,4\}
; we handle these cases separately.

Suppose first that
|m|=3
, and note that the maximality of the rank of
\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)=(E^{1}_{-2m,7})_{2}
implies that
r=3
as well and thus
m=e=(1,1,1)
. By the maximality of rank and the usual cellular computation of this relative homology group as in Lemma
5.3.1
, we find that
w_{0}^{C}(2m)=-2
,
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{i})=-1
for all
1\leq i\leq r
, and
w_{0}^{C}(2m+e^{i}+e^{j})=0
for all
1\leq i<j\leq r
.

Since
(C,o)
has three smooth branches, we can see that the restriction
x
to
(C,o)
of a generic linear function germ on the ambient smooth germ satisfies
\mathfrak{v}_{1}(x)=\mathfrak{v}_{2}(x)=\mathfrak{v}_{3}(x)=1
; by taking an appropriate change of coordinates for
{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cong\mathbb{C}\{t_{1}\}\times\mathbb{C}\{t_{2}\}\times\mathbb{C}\{t_{3}\}
, we then have
x=(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\in\mathscr{O}
. Since
w_{0}^{C}((3,2,3))=w_{0}^{C}((2,2,3))+1
, Lemma
2.2.5
gives us an element
y_{1}\in\mathscr{O}
with
\mathfrak{v}_{1}(y_{1})=2
,
\mathfrak{v}_{2}(y_{1})\geq 2
, and
\mathfrak{v}_{3}(y_{1})\geq 3
, which we abbreviate by
\mathfrak{v}(y_{1})=(2,\geq 2,\geq 3)
. By the symmetry of our statements under coordinate permutation, we likewise obtain
y_{2}\in\mathscr{O}
with
\mathfrak{v}(y_{2})=(\geq 2,2,\geq 3)
; letting
y_{12}\in\mathscr{O}
be a generic linear combination of
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
, we then have
\mathfrak{v}(y_{12})=(2,2,\geq 3)
. Using symmetry again, we obtain
y_{23},y_{31}\in\mathscr{O}
with
\mathfrak{v}(y_{23})=(\geq 3,2,2)
and
\mathfrak{v}(y_{31})=(2,\geq 3,2)
. We take
y\in\mathscr{O}
to be a generic linear combination of
y_{12}
,
y_{23}
, and
y_{31}
, so that, if
y=(\psi_{1}(t_{1}),\psi_{2}(t_{2}),\psi_{3}(t_{3}))=(a_{1}{t_{1}}^{2}+\cdots,a_{2}{t_{2}}^{2}+\cdots,a_{3}{t_{3}}^{2}+\cdots)
, the coefficients
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
are distinct. Letting
\mathscr{O}_{T}\subseteq\mathscr{O}
be the convergent power series subring generated by
x
and
y
, we find that
\mathscr{O}_{T}\cong\mathbb{C}\{x,y\}/(f)
, where
f=\prod_{i=1}^{3}(y-\psi_{i}(x))
. Since this is the local ring of a
T_{3,6}
singularity and the composed map
\mathscr{O}_{T}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{O}\hookrightarrow{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}
is birational over each branch, we can then see that
(C,o)
birationally dominates a parabolic singularity, which gives the result by Proposition
4.4.1
.

The argument if
|m|=4
is similar; again we find
|m|=r
and
m=e
, and now, for each
I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}
, we have
w_{0}^{C}(m+\sum_{i\in I}e^{i})=|I|-2
. Our prior strategy gives us elements
x,y\in\mathscr{O}\subset{\overline{\mathscr{O}}}\cong\mathbb{C}\{t_{1}\}\times\mathbb{C}\{t_{2}\}\times\mathbb{C}\{t_{3}\}\times\mathbb{C}\{t_{4}\}
with
x=(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4})
and
y=(\psi_{1}(t_{1}),\psi_{2}(t_{2}),\psi_{3}(t_{3}),\psi_{4}(t_{4}))=(a_{1}t_{1}+\cdots,a_{2}t_{2}+\cdots,a_{3}t_{3}+\cdots,a_{4}t_{4}+\cdots)
such that the coefficients
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}
are distinct; as before, the subring
\mathscr{O}_{T}\subseteq\mathscr{O}
given by
\mathbb{C}\{x,y\}/(f)
with
f=\prod_{i=1}^{4}(y-\psi_{i}(x))
witnesses the fact that
(C,o)
birationally dominates a parabolic singularity.
∎

10.3.
Proof of Theorem
2

We are now nearly in a position to prove our theorem; first we will need the following additional lemma:

Lemma 10.3.1
.

Suppose that
|m|=3
. Then
(C,o)
has a minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight
0
if and only if
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)\geq 3-r
. Moreover, if these conditions hold,
(C,o)
is of finite CM type.

Proof.

Note that
r\leq|m|=3
; we will consider each case separately, although we first note in general that
w_{0}^{C}(m)=-1
by (
2.2.6
). First, if
r=1
, it is clear by Lemma
5.3.1
that
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)=0
, and likewise, by (
2.2.4
and
m\in\mathscr{S}
,
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)=w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1})=0
whereas
3-r=3-1=2
. Thus the two statements are equivalent by virtue of both being false.

If
r=2
, we can see by Lemmas
5.3.1
and
5.3.2
that
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)\neq 0
implies
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)=w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1}+e^{2})=1
. On the other hand, if
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)=0
, we must have
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)=w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1}+e^{2})=-1
by (
2.2.4
); hence, as desired,
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)\neq 0
if and only if
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)
is bounded below by
3-r=3-2=1
. Since
|m|=3
, we can also see that, for some
i\in\{1,2\}
,
2m=m+e+e^{i}
, so this further implies that
w_{0}^{C}(2m)\geq 0
by (
2.2.4
) and thus
(C,o)
has finite CM type by Proposition
7.1.1
.

Finally, if
r=3
, we proceed as follows. If
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)\neq 0
and so there exist indices
1\leq i<j\leq r
such that
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i}+e^{j})=1
by Lemmas
5.3.1
and
5.3.2
, we can obtain
m+e
from
m+e^{i}+e^{j}
by adding the final basis element, and so
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)\geq 3-r=3-3=0
follows by (
2.2.4
). On the other hand, if
w_{0}^{C}(m+e)\geq 0
, then the fact that
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{i})=0
for all
1\leq i\leq r
by (
2.2.4
) and
m\in\mathscr{S}
can be used with (
2.2.8
) to guarantee that at most one of
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1}+e^{2})
,
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{1}+e^{3})
, and
w_{0}^{C}(m+e^{2}+e^{3})
, which are all
\pm 1
by (
2.2.4
), can be
-1
. Our usual cellular computation then shows that the other two 2-cubes are already enough to trivialize the relative first homology, so
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)=0
. The desired equivalence follows; we can also see that, under these conditions,
w_{0}^{C}(2m)=w_{0}^{C}(m+e)\geq 0
, so
(C,o)
is of finite CM type by Proposition
7.1.1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2
.

By Proposition
10.1.1
, tameness implies that
\min w_{0}^{C}=-2
; therefore, for the first claim, it suffices to establish that our stated conditions are equivalent to the overring conditions of Proposition
4.4.2
under the assumption that this equality holds. (O1a) will always be satisfied in this setting by (
2.2.6
), while Lemma
9.2.1
and Proposition
8.1.1
together imply that our Condition (c) on the branches
C_{i}
is equivalent to (O1b).

Both sets of conditions imply that
r>1
; in the case of the theorem’s hypotheses, this can be deduced from, e.g., Lemma
5.3.1
and Condition (b) on the existence of a minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight
-1
, while it is due to (O1b) and the supposition that
(C,o)
is not of finite CM type in the case of the overring conditions. As such, we find by Proposition
10.1.2
that, in our setting, Condition (b) is equivalent to both (O2a) and (O3) being satisfied.

Finally, we can see that Condition (d) on the unions
\hat{C}_{i}
is equivalent to (O2b) in our setting. Since both sets of conditions imply that the multiplicity of
C
at its closed point is at most 4, and removing a branch reduces the total multiplicity by the multiplicity of that branch, each
\hat{C}_{i}
has multiplicity at most 3. For indices
1\leq i\leq r
where this bound is strict, it follows by Proposition
8.1.1
and Remark
9.2.3
that
\hat{C}_{i}
both dominates an
A_{n}
curve germ for some
n\geq 0
and satisfies the weight inequality of Lemma
9.2.4
. On the other hand, if this multiplicity is exactly 3, we can see that this weight inequality is equivalent to
\hat{C}_{i}
having a minimal spectral 1-cycle of weight
0
and overall minimum weight
-1
by Lemma
10.3.1
and Theorem
7.1.2
, and that this in turn is equivalent to
\hat{C}_{i}
dominating a
D_{n}
singularity for some
n\geq 4
by Proposition
8.2.1
. Thus the first claim follows by Lemma
9.2.4
.

The second claim is given by Propositions
10.2.1
and
10.2.2
.
∎

11.
The motivic Poincaré series

Let
(C,o)
be a complex-analytic curve germ; we use the conventions of Sections
2
and
5
. In the main characterizations of the previous sections, the lattice-homological invariant
\min w_{0}^{C}
and certain pieces of information about the shape of the
E^{1}
-page of the associated spectral sequence play a crucial role. Using the connections between the refinement of the
E^{1}
-page given in Proposition
5.2.2
and the
multivariable motivic Poincaré series
associated with
(C,o)
, we are now in a position to describe the Cohen-Macaulay type of curve germs in terms of this latter object; indeed, we will see that an appropriately-defined univariate version of this series is enough for this purpose. Despite the strength of the motivic Poincaré series as a numerical invariant, the existence of such a description is somewhat surprising in light of the lack of an obvious conceptual connection with the classification of indecomposable CM
\mathscr{O}
-modules.

11.1.
The Poincaré series of the (refined)
E^{1}
-page (
[
29
,
49
]
)

In this subsection, we collect some additional facts and information regarding the entries
(E^{1}_{-\ell,q})_{-2n}
defined in Proposition
5.2.2
, which may give additional insight into their properties and role. These were already considered (in a slightly different way) in
[
29
]
, where they served as a bridge between the (local) lattice homology associated with the lattice point
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
(in some sense, the lattice homology in the rectangle
R(\ell,\ell+e)
) and the Orlik-Solomon algebra associated with the local hyperplane arrangement
\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}\mathscr{F}(\ell+e^{i})\subseteq\mathscr{F}(\ell)
. By Theorems 4.2.1 and 5.3.1 of
[
29
]
, reinterpreted in the present notation in Theorems 4.7.3 and 6.1.3 of
[
49
]
, one has the following properties for a given
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
:

(11.1.1)

(E^{1}_{-\ell,|\ell|+k})_{-2n}=H_{k}(S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{-\ell},S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{-\ell}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-|\ell|-1})\text{ has no $\mathbb{Z}$-torsion.}

(11.1.2)

\text{If }(E^{1}_{-\ell,|\ell|+k})_{-2n}=H_{k}(S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{-\ell},S_{n}\cap\mathfrak{Y}_{-\ell}\cap\mathfrak{X}_{-|\ell|-1})\neq 0,\text{ then }n=w_{0}^{C}(\ell)+k.

(We note that (
11.1.2
) also follows immediately from Lemma
5.3.1
, while (
11.1.1
) can be deduced from its proof.)

The generating function in
\mathbb{Z}[[{\bf T},Q]]_{Q}[h]
for the terms
\rk{(E^{1}_{-\ell,|\ell|+k})_{-2n}}
(cf.
[
49
]
, noting the change in grading convention) is

(11.1.3)

PE({\bf T},Q,h)=PE(T_{1},\ldots,T_{r},Q,h):=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r},k\in\mathbb{N},n\in\mathbb{Z}}\rk{(E^{1}_{-\ell,|\ell|+k})_{-2n}}\cdot{T_{1}}^{\ell_{1}}\cdots{T_{r}}^{\ell_{r}}Q^{n}h^{k}.

By making the substitutions
T_{i}\to T
, we obtain also a univariate version, the generating function of the terms
\rk{(E_{-d,d+k}^{1})_{-2n}}
:

(11.1.4)

PE(T,Q,h):=PE({\bf T},Q,h)|_{T_{i}\to T}=\sum_{d,k\in\mathbb{N},n\in\mathbb{Z}}\rk{(E^{1}_{-d,d+k})_{-2n}}\cdot T^{d}Q^{n}h^{k}.

Note in particular that this does not depend on the refinement given in Proposition
5.2.2
, only on the original
E^{1}
-page of the spectral sequence.

11.2.
Relation with the motivic Poincaré series

The
multivariable motivic Poincaré series

P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}\mathfrak{p}^{m}_{\ell}(q){\bf t}^{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}[[{\bf t}]][q]
can be defined in many different ways—e.g., via motivic integrals or generating functions with coefficients in the Grothendieck ring of algebraic varieties as in
[
15
]
. Here we define it via the Hilbert function of
(C,o)
as follows (cf.
[
15
]
):

(11.2.1)

\mathfrak{p}^{m}_{\ell}(q):=\sum_{J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}}(-1)^{|J|}\cdot\frac{q^{\mathfrak{h}(\ell+e^{J})}}{1-q}=\sum_{J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}}(-1)^{|J|}\cdot\frac{q^{\mathfrak{h}(\ell+e^{J})}-q^{\mathfrak{h}(\ell)}}{1-q},

with
e^{J}
as in (
6.1.1
).

We will also find it useful to consider the corresponding
univariable motivic Poincaré
series
P_{C}^{m}(t,q):=P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)|_{t_{i}\to t}
, which, to the best of our knowledge, has not been explicitly discussed in the literature; we denote its coefficient polynomials by
\mathfrak{p}^{m}_{d}(q):=\sum_{|\ell|=d}\mathfrak{p}^{m}_{\ell}(q)
, so that
P_{C}^{m}(t,q)=\sum_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{m}_{d}(q)t^{d}
.

Proposition 11.2.2
.

[
15
,
41
,
42
,
28
,
29
]

We now recall a few key properties of
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)
:

(a)

\lim_{q\to 1}P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)=P_{C}({\bf t})
.

(b)

P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)
is a rational function of type
\overline{P_{C}^{m}}({\bf t},q)/(\prod_{i=1}^{r}(1-t_{i}q))
with
\overline{P_{C}^{m}}({\bf t},q)\in\mathbb{Z}[{\bf t},q]
.

(c)

The support of
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)
as an element of
(\mathbb{Z}[q])[[{\bf t}]]
is exactly
\mathscr{S}_{C}
. That is, for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{m}_{\ell}(q)\neq 0
if and only if
\ell\in\mathscr{S}_{C}
.

(d)

If
(C,o)
is Gorenstein then, for
c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{r})
the conductor,

\overline{P_{C}^{m}}((qt_{1})^{-1},\ldots,(qt_{r})^{-1},q)=q^{-\delta(C,o)}\prod_{i=1}^{r}{t_{i}}^{-c_{i}}\cdot\overline{P_{C}^{m}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r},q).

For a combinatorial formula, valid for plane curve singularities, in terms of the embedded resolution graph, see
[
15
]
, or
[
28
]
for a simplified version.

Proposition 11.2.3
.

The key identification of
PE({\bf T},Q,h)
and
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)
is as follows:

(a)

(
[
49
, Theorem 6.1.3]
; see also
[
29
]
)
PE({\bf T},Q,h)|_{T_{i}\to t_{i}\sqrt{q},\,Q\to\sqrt{q},\,h\to-\sqrt{q}}=P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q).
(In particular, by (
11.1.2
), we can see that this substitution takes the monomial
{\bf T}^{\ell}Q^{n}h^{k}
to
(-1)^{k}{\bf t}^{\ell}q^{(|\ell|+n+k)/2}=(-1)^{k}{\bf t}^{\ell}q^{\mathfrak{h}(\ell)+k}
for
(\ell,n,k)\in\supp PE\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{N}
.)

(b)

The preceding substitution is injective on monomials in the support of
PE
; that is, if
{\bf T}^{\ell}Q^{n}h^{k}
and
{\bf T}^{\ell^{\prime}}Q^{n^{\prime}}h^{k^{\prime}}
are distinct and have nonzero coefficients in
PE({\bf T},Q,h)
, the substitution sends them to different monomials of
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)
.

Proof.

Part (b) follows from Part (a) and (
11.1.2
).
∎

In particular, Part (a) implies that the corresponding substitution identity holds for the univariable series as well; that is,
PE(T,Q,h)|_{T\to t\sqrt{q},\,Q\to\sqrt{q},\,h\to-\sqrt{q}}=P_{C}^{m}(t,q)
.

Corollary 11.2.4
.

We list some further consequences:

(a)

If
(E^{1}_{-\ell,|\ell|+k})_{-2n}\neq 0
for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
k=n-w_{0}^{C}(\ell)
and the rank of this free
\mathbb{Z}
-module is
(-1)^{k}
times the coefficient of
{\bf t}^{\ell}q^{\mathfrak{h}(\ell)+k}
in
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)
. For example:

(i)

If
|m|=3
, then
\rk\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}(C,o)=\rk{(E_{-3,4}^{1})_{0}}=\rk{(E_{-m,1+|m|}^{1})_{-2(0)}}
is the negative of the coefficient of
{\bf t}^{m}q^{2}
.

(ii)

If
|m|=3
, then
\rk\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)=\rk{(E_{-6,7}^{1})_{2}}=\rk{(E_{-2m,1+2|m|}^{1})_{-2(-1)}}
is either zero or the negative of the coefficient of
{\bf t}^{2m}q^{3}
, whichever is larger.

(iii)

If
|m|=4
, then
\rk\mathfrak{M}_{1,-1}(C,o)=\rk{(E_{-4,5}^{1})_{2}}=\rk{(E_{-m,1+|m|}^{1})_{-2(-1)}}
is the negative of the coefficient of
{\bf t}^{m}q^{2}
.

(b)

\min w_{0}^{C}=\ord P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)|_{t_{i}\to\omega^{-1},\,q\to\omega^{2}}=\ord\overline{P_{C}^{m}}({\bf t},q)|_{t_{i}\to\omega^{-1},\,q\to\omega^{2}}
and the coefficient of the lowest-order term in each case is
\rk\big((\mathbb{H}_{0}(C,o))_{-2\min w_{0}^{C}}\big)
.

Proof.

The main statement in Part (a) follows directly from the proposition. Each of the consequences (i–iii) is then immediate in the case where the module in question has nonzero rank; otherwise, Part (b) of the proposition no longer applies, and so we must account for the possibility that a different term of
PE({\bf T},Q,h)
produces the same monomial in
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)
. For (i) and (iii), this is already clear since
\mathfrak{h}(m)=1
necessarily and so we must have
k=1
and
n=3-|m|
for the term
{\bf t}^{m}q^{2}
to appear. For (ii), we note that
\mathfrak{h}(2m)\geq 2
, so we can obtain
{\bf t}^{2m}q^{3}
either by
\mathfrak{h}(2m)=2,k=1,n=-1
or by
\mathfrak{h}(2m)=3,k=0,n=0
; if
\rk(E^{1}_{-2m,6})_{0}>0
, however, we can see by the main statement of Part (a) and
k=0
that the resulting coefficient of
{\bf t}^{2m}q^{3}
will be positive as well. The result follows.

For Part (b), we proceed as follows. By Part (a), if
(E^{1}_{-\ell,|\ell|+k})_{-2n}\neq 0
for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the monomial
{\bf T}^{\ell}Q^{n}h^{k}
will be sent by the composition of our two substitutions to
\omega^{w_{0}^{C}(\ell)+2k}
, which has order
w_{0}^{C}(\ell)+2k\geq w_{0}^{C}(\ell)\geq\min w_{0}^{C}
. (The fact that
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)|_{t_{i}\to\omega^{-1},\,q\to\omega^{2}}
is a well-defined element of
\mathbb{Z}[[\omega]]_{\omega}
now follows by Theorem
5.1.5
and (
2.2.4
).) Moreover, if
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
is such that
w_{0}^{C}(\ell)=\min w_{0}^{C}
, we can see by (
2.2.4
) that
\rk{(E^{1}_{-\ell,|\ell|+0})_{-2w_{0}^{C}(\ell)}}=1
, so the term
\omega^{\min w_{0}^{C}}
will have coefficient
\sum_{w_{0}^{C}(\ell)=\min w_{0}^{C}}(-1)^{0}=\#\left\{\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}\mid w_{0}^{C}(\ell)=\min w_{0}^{C}\right\}=\rk\mathbb{H}_{0,-2\min w_{0}^{C}}(C,o)>0
in the substituted series. The first result for
P_{C}^{m}
now follows; for
\overline{P_{C}^{m}}
, observe that the substitution takes each
t_{i}q
to
\omega
.
∎

Example 11.2.5
.

Suppose
(C,o)
is the
D_{4}
singularity, so that
m=(1,1,1)
. Then, using either (
11.2.1
) and the Hilbert function computed in Subsection
6.5
or
[
28
, 5.1]
, we have

P^{m}_{C}({\bf t},q)=1+\frac{q(1-q)^{2}t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}-q^{3}t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}(1-t_{1})(1-t_{2})(1-t_{3})}{(1-t_{1}q)(1-t_{2}q)(1-t_{3}q)}

and hence
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)|_{t_{i}\to\omega^{-1},\,q\to\omega^{2}}=\frac{1+3\omega-4\omega^{2}+2\omega^{3}}{\omega(1-\omega)^{2}}
. Since the coefficient of
{\bf t}^{m}
is
q(1-q)^{2}-q^{3}=q-2q^{2}
, we find that the coefficient of
{\bf t}^{m}q^{2}
is
-2
, which agrees with the computation
(E^{1}_{-3,4})_{0}=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
of Subsection
6.5
. Likewise, since
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)|_{t_{i}\to\omega^{-1},\,q\to\omega^{2}}=\omega^{-1}+\cdots
, we find as in our computations that
\min w_{0}^{C}=-1
is achieved at a single lattice point.

Remark 11.2.6
.

In Subsection
6.1
, we mentioned that (for non-plane curve singularities) the ordinary multivariable Poincaré series does not determine the Hilbert function. However, the multivariable
motivic
Poincaré series does determine the Hilbert function.

Indeed, for any
\ell\in\mathscr{S}
, the coefficient
\mathfrak{p}_{\ell}^{m}(q)
of
{\bf t}^{\ell}
in
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)
has the form
\sum_{k\geq 0}p_{\ell,k}q^{\mathfrak{h}(\ell)+k}
, where
p_{\ell,0}\neq 0
; on the other hand, for
\ell\not\in\mathscr{S}
,
[
49
, Theorem 6.1.3(h)]
tells us that every corresponding summand in the improved
E^{1}
-page is zero, and so by Proposition
11.2.3

\mathfrak{p}_{\ell}^{m}(q)=0
as well. Hence we can retrieve the semigroup, which already gives us the Hilbert function by (
2.2.3
). More directly, we note that by the preceding formula
\mathfrak{h}(\ell)=\ord\mathfrak{p}_{\ell}^{m}(q)
for
\ell\in\mathscr{S}
, and for arbitrary
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}
we have
\mathfrak{h}(\ell)=\mathfrak{h}(s(\ell))
, where
s(\ell)
is the minimal semigroup element satisfying
s(\ell)\geq\ell
, by (
2.2.3
); that is,
\mathfrak{h}(\ell)=\ord\mathfrak{p}_{\min\{\ell^{\prime}\geq\ell\mid\mathfrak{p}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m}(q)\neq 0\}}^{m}(q)
.

11.3.
The main characterizations reformulated in terms of
P^{m}_{C}

We now obtain the following reformulation of our main results in terms of the univariable motivic Poincaré series
P_{C}^{m}(t,q)=\sum_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{p}^{m}_{d}(q)t^{d}
:

Theorem 3
.

Let
f^{C}(\omega):=P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)|_{t_{i}\to\omega^{-1},\,q\to\omega^{2}}=P_{C}^{m}(t,q)|_{t\to\omega^{-1},\,q\to\omega^{2}}
. For convenience, we also write
\mathfrak{p}^{m}_{d}(q)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\pi_{d,j}^{C}q^{j}
and set
\mu^{C}:=\ord_{t}(P_{C}^{m}(t,q)-\mathfrak{p}^{C}_{0}(q))=\min\{d\geq 1\mid\mathfrak{p}^{C}_{d}(q)\neq 0\}
.

Then
(C,o)
is of finite Cohen-Macaulay type if and only if
\ord f^{C}\geq-1
. In particular:

•

(C,o)
birationally dominates (and hence is) a simple germ of type
A_{n}
(for some
n\geq 0
) if and only if
\ord f^{C}=0
or, equivalently,
\mu^{C}\leq 2
.

•

(C,o)
birationally dominates a simple singularity of type
D_{n}
(for some
n\geq 4
), but no simple germ of type
A_{n}
(for any
n\geq 0
), if and only if
\ord f^{C}=-1
and
\pi_{3,2}^{C}\neq 0
(equivalently,
\pi_{3,2}^{C}<0
).

•

(C,o)
birationally dominates a simple singularity of type
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
, or
E_{8}
, but no simple germ of type
A_{n}
(for any
n\geq 0
) or
D_{n}
(for any
n\geq 4
), if and only if
\ord f^{C}=-1
and
\pi_{3,2}^{C}=0
.

Likewise,
(C,o)
is of tame Cohen-Macaulay type if and only if all of the following conditions hold:

(a)

\ord f^{C}=-2
.

(b)

Either
\mu^{C}=3
and
\pi_{6,3}^{C}<0
, or
\mu^{C}=4
and
\pi_{4,3}^{C}\neq 0
(equivalently,
\pi_{4,3}^{C}<0
).

(c)

For each
1\leq i\leq r
,
\ord f^{C_{i}}=0
(equivalently,
\mu^{C_{i}}\leq 2
).

(d)

For each
1\leq i\leq r
, if we let
(\hat{C}_{i},o):=\bigcup_{j\neq i}(C_{j},o)
, then either
\ord f^{\hat{C}_{i}}=0
, or
\ord f^{\hat{C}_{i}}=-1
and
\pi_{3,2}^{\hat{C}_{i}}\neq 0
.

If Conditions (a–d) hold,
(C,o)
is of finite growth if and only if either
\mu^{C}=3
and
\pi_{6,3}^{C}=-2
, or
\mu^{C}=4
and
\pi_{4,3}^{C}=-3
. (Note that, in general,
\mu^{C}=3
implies
\pi_{6,3}^{C}\geq-2
and
\mu^{C}=4
implies
\pi_{4,3}^{C}\geq-3
.)

Proof.

As a first step, we note that the reasoning of Remark
11.2.6
and the containment
\mathscr{S}\setminus\{0\}\subseteq m+\mathbb{N}^{r}
together imply that
\mu^{C}=|m|
.

The statements on finite CM type will now follow from Theorem
1
; the general characterization in terms of
\ord f^{C}
is immediate from Corollary
11.2.4
(b), and the statement on
A_{n}
-dominance follows from this and our characterization of
\mu^{C}
. For the statements on
D_{n}
and
E_{n}
, it is enough to note, again by Remark
11.2.6
and the containment
\mathscr{S}\setminus\{0\}\subseteq m+\mathbb{N}^{r}
, that any nonzero term of
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)
sent by the substitution
t_{i}\to t
to one of
t
-degree
|m|
is a multiple of
{\bf t}^{m}
; since
\min w_{0}^{C}=\ord f^{C}=-1
and so
|m|=3
in these cases, the statements are now immediate from the theorem by Corollary
11.2.4
(a)(i).

For the tameness statement, we note that each of our Conditions (a–d) is equivalent to the corresponding condition of Theorem
2
. For (a), (c), and (d), this is immediate from our preceding discussion, and likewise (b) follows in the case
\mu^{C}=4
by the above reasoning and Corollary
11.2.4
(a)(iii). The reasoning for (b) in the case
\mu^{C}=3
is similar, but the argument concerning the relationship between
{\bf t}^{2m}q^{3}
and
t^{2|m|}q^{3}
is more complicated. By Lemma
5.3.2
, any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}^{r}\setminus\{2m\}
such that a nonzero term with
{\bf t}
-exponent
\ell
is sent by our substitution to
t^{2|m|}q^{3}
must satisfy
\mathfrak{h}(\ell)=3
; hence the term must arise from a monomial
{\bf T}^{\ell}Q^{n}h^{k}
with
k=0
in
PE({\bf T},Q,h)
under the substitution of Proposition
11.2.3
and so must have a positive coefficient. Thus, if
t^{2|m|}q^{3}
has a negative coefficient, it must be the case that
(E_{-6,7}^{1})_{2}\neq 0
by Corollary
11.2.4
(a)(ii); on the other hand, if
(E_{-6,7}^{1})_{2}\neq 0
, then Lemma
5.3.6
and Remark
11.2.6
imply that no such
\ell
can exist to begin with, so
11.2.4
(a)(ii) gives us that the coefficient of
t^{2|m|}q^{3}
is
-\rk{(E_{-6,7}^{1})_{2}}
, as desired.

The statement on finite growth follows from the corresponding statement of Theorem 2 by the identifications we have already established, and we likewise obtain the parenthetical statement on bounds from the discussion motivating Definition
5.3.7
.
∎

Note that the appropriate reformulation of Remark
1.0.1
holds for these conditions as well.

Remark 11.3.1
.

The preceding theorem tells us that the CM type of
(C,o)
can be deduced not just from the usual multivariable motivic Poincaré series
P_{C}^{m}({\bf t},q)
,
P_{C_{i}}^{m}({\bf t},q)
, and
P_{\hat{C}_{i}}^{m}({\bf t},q)
, but even from the univariate versions
P_{C}^{m}(t,q)
,
P_{C_{i}}^{m}(t,q)
, and
P_{\hat{C}_{i}}^{m}(t,q)
. Thus, in these circumstances, we find that the univariable motivic Poincaré polynomial captures the pertinent information about its multivariate counterpart while being potentially easier to compute (insofar as it requires only the information of the spectral sequence’s
E^{1}
-page simpliciter, without the direct sum decomposition); it would be interesting to determine the extent to which these observations hold true in other applications. Likewise, the significance of the substituted series
P_{C}^{m}(t,q)|_{t\to\omega^{-1},\,q\to\omega^{2}}
, particularly its relationship to the lattice homology, seems worthy of further investigation; however, we leave such matters to future work.

12.
Plane curve singularities. Further remarks and examples

12.1.

As discussed in Subsection
3.1
, the classification of reduced plane curve singularity germs (i.e., hypersurface singularity germs of corank at most 2) of
\mathscr{R}
-modality at most 2 can be found in
[
5
]
. Our results thus far suggest that this classification can be reframed in terms of lattice-homological invariants; here we sketch the beginnings of such a description, leaving the project of elaborating on them systematically to future work.

The simple plane curve singularity germs—that is, those with
\mathscr{R}
-modality 0—are precisely the
ADE
singularities, and so Corollary
7.1.3
shows that these can be characterized as the plane curve singularity germs such that
\min w_{0}\geq-1
. Indeed, we can see by the computations of Section
6
that, as in Theorem
1
, we can use the lattice homology and spectral sequence to obtain a more granular classification; the
A_{n}
singularities are precisely the plane curve singularities such that
\min w_{0}=0
, the
D_{n}
are those such that
\min w_{0}=-1
and
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}\neq 0
, and the
E_{n}
are those such that
\min w_{0}=-1
and
\mathfrak{M}_{1,0}=0
.

We recall that the
\mathscr{R}
-unimodal germs are grouped into the parabolic, hyperbolic, and exceptional families, with the
T_{pq}
germs comprising the first two. We have the following characterizations:

Lemma 12.1.1
.

Let
(C,o)\subset(\mathbb{C}^{2},o)
be a reduced plane curve germ. Then:

•

(C,o)
is
\mathscr{R}
-unimodal parabolic if and only if it is tame and of finite growth.

•

(C,o)
is
\mathscr{R}
-unimodal hyperbolic if and only if it is tame and of infinite growth.

•

(C,o)
is
\mathscr{R}
-unimodal exceptional if and only if it is not tame but satisfies
\min w_{0}=-2
(equivalently,
\min w_{0}\leq-2
) and
\delta(C,o)=\eu(\mathbb{H}_{*}(C,o))\leq 7
(equivalently,
\delta\in\{6,7\}
).

(The tameness and growth conditions can be phrased in terms of lattice homology groups and groups of minimal spectral cycles as per Theorem
2
; we use the present formulation for the sake of brevity.)

Proof.

Schappert proved in
[
51
]
that the plane curve singularities of modality 2 or larger are not tame; consequently, verifying that none of the exceptional singularities are tame is enough to establish that the parabolic and hyperbolic germs are the only tame plane curve singularities. Recall from
[
5
]
that the list of all such exceptional germs is given by
E_{12},E_{13},E_{14},Z_{11},Z_{12},Z_{13},W_{12},W_{13}
, where the subscripts give the curves’ Milnor numbers; to see that these are not tame, we make the following observations about the conditions of Theorem
2
, largely by direct inspection of their equations:

•

E_{12}
,
E_{14}
, and
W_{12}
are unibranch, and so Condition (b) fails.
E_{13}
has two branches, but a lattice computation shows that Condition (b) fails in this case as well.

•

Each of
Z_{11}
and
W_{13}
is the union of a smooth branch with an
E_{6}
singularity, so Condition (c) fails. Likewise, one the branches of
Z_{13}
is
E_{8}
, which also violates Condition (c).

•

Removing one of the three branches of
Z_{12}
gives us
E_{7}
, so Condition (d) fails.

To distinguish between the parabolic and hyperbolic singularities, it suffices to show that no hyperbolic singularity birationally dominates a parabolic one by Proposition
4.4.1
. Since the
T_{4,4}
and
T_{3,6}
singularities have 4 and 3 branches respectively with both delta invariants equal to 6, Lemma
3.2.2
(b) and (f) together exclude this possibility in all cases except
T_{4,5}
; since this is a 4-branch singularity, it is now enough to adapt the reasoning of Example
3.2.3
(1) to show that it cannot dominate
T_{4,4}
. This concludes the proof of the first two characterizations.

For the third, recall that, for a reduced plane curve germ with
r
irreducible components and Milnor number
\mu
,
2\delta=\mu+r-1
(see, e.g.,
[
39
]
); thus it can be seen that
E_{12}
,
Z_{11}
, and
W_{12}
have
\delta=6
and the other exceptional singularities have
\delta=7
. Moreover, a computation of the sort used in Section
6
shows that in fact
\mathbb{H}_{*}(E_{12},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(T_{3,6},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(T_{3,7},o)
,
\mathbb{H}_{*}(E_{13},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(E_{14},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(T_{3,8},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(T_{3,9},o)
,
\mathbb{H}_{*}(Z_{11},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(W_{12},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(T_{4,4},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(T_{4,5},o)
, and
\mathbb{H}_{*}(Z_{12},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(Z_{13},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(W_{13},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(T_{4,6},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(T_{4,7},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(T_{5,6},o)\cong\mathbb{H}_{*}(T_{5,7},o)
, so the exceptional germs have
\min w_{0}=-2
as well by Theorem
2
. Since we have already argued these germs are not tame, the forward direction follows. To establish the reverse, it suffices to note that, in the classification of
[
5
]
, all bimodal plane curve germs have Milnor number at least 15. By the upper semicontinuity of the Milnor number and the density of the bimodal germs in the space of plane curve singularities of modality at least 2, it follows that the same holds true for all such germs. Therefore, we have
\delta\geq\lceil\mu/2\rceil\geq 8
in these cases, which gives the desired result since the condition
\min w_{0}=-2
excludes the simple germs by Theorem
1
.
∎

Remark 12.1.2
.

We make some observations:

(a)

Although the exceptional unimodal germs are characterized among the non-tame plane curve germs of infinite CM type by the condition
\delta\leq 7
, and indeed this inequality holds for the parabolic germs as well, no such bound applies to the hyperbolic germs. In the setting of Subsection
6.10
, for example, the equality
|c|=2\delta
and our computations give us
\delta=a+b+4
, which can be made arbitrarily large by taking appropriate values of
a
and
b
.

(b)

The exceptional germs can be used to illustrate the necessity of the various conditions of Theorem
2
; for example, the isomorphisms of lattice homology groups discussed in the preceding proof show that lattice homology alone cannot detect tameness. Likewise, we can see by computations that, e.g.,
Z_{11}
satisfies Condition (b) as well as Condition (a), so knowing the group of minimal spectral 1-cycles of weight
-1
of the curve in question as well is also insufficient; that is, Conditions (c) and (d) cannot be dispensed with.

(c)

The condition
\min w_{0}=-2
is not enough to guarantee (
\mathscr{R}
-)unimodality; for example, the
W_{1,0}
singularity defined by
x^{4}+y^{6}+(a_{0}+a_{1}y)x^{2}y^{3}
with
{a_{0}}^{2}\neq 4
(
[
5
]
) is
\mathscr{R}
-bimodal but has
\min w_{0}=-2
,
\mu=15
, and
\delta=8
.

(d)

On the other hand, not every bimodal germ has
\min w_{0}=-2
; for example, the
E_{18}
singularity given by
x^{3}+y^{10}+(a_{0}+a_{1}y)xy^{7}
(
[
5
]
) has
\min w_{0}=-3
,
\mu=18
, and
\delta=9
.

(e)

The family of
\mathscr{K}\!\!
-unimodal germs is much larger than the
\mathscr{R}
-unimodal one, and we can see that is not subject to the same weight condition; for example, the
W_{18}
singularity of
[
5
]
is
\mathscr{K}\!\!
-unimodal with
\min w_{0}=-3
.
